Ὅθεν καὶ διαφόρως ἐξορισθεὶς παρὰ τῶν αἱρετικῶν βασιλέων, καὶ φυλακαῖς παραδοθεὶς, καὶ ἐν αὐταῖς τῇ κακουχίᾳ ταλαιπωρηθείς, ἐν καλῇ ὁμολογίᾳ ἐτελειώθη, ταφεὶς ὑπὸ τοῦ μαθητοῦ αὐτοῦ.
Τῇ αὐτῇ ἡμέρᾳ Ἄθλησις τοῦ ἁγίου μάρτυρος Φιλουμένου. Φιλούμενος ὁ μάρτυς ἦν ἐπὶ Αὐρηλιανοῦ τοῦ βασιλέως, ἀπὸ χώρας τῆς Λυκαονίας, πραγματευόμενος, καὶ σῖτον κομίζων ἐν τῇ χώρᾳ τῆς Ἀγκύρας τῆς Γαλατίας. Χριστιανὸς δὲ ὤν, διαβάλλεται τῷ ἄρχοντι Ἀγκύρας. Καὶ παραστὰς τῷ κριτηρίῳ αὐτοῦ, καὶ τὸν Χριστὸν ὁμολογήσας, Θεὸν εἶναι τοῦ παντός, καὶ Κύριον, πρῶτον μὲν ἡλοῦται τοὺς πόδας καὶ τὰς χεῖρας ἥλοις σιδηροῖς, καὶ βασανίζεται. Ἔπειτα κρεμασθείς, μετὰ τοῦ σπαθίου ξέεται, καὶ τοῦ ξύλου ἀποκαταδιασθεὶς, εἰς φοῦρνον ἐκπυρωθέντα ἐμβάλλεται. Ἐκ τούτου δὲ, τῇ τοῦ Θεοῦ δυνάμει, ἀβλαβὴς διαφυλαχθείς, κατηλοῦται αὖθις τοὺς πόδας, καὶ τὰς χεῖρας, καὶ τὴν κεφαλὴν· καὶ ἐπὶ τριάκοντα σταδίους ἐλαύνεται· ἕως λιποψυχήσας ἀπὸ τοῦ πολλοῦ δρόμου, καὶ τοῦ τοσούτου κόπου, τὴν μακρίαν αὐτοῦ ψυχὴν παρέδωκε τῷ Θεῷ, δι᾿ ὃν ἐμαρτύρησεν.
Eodem die 218 Certamen sancti martyris Irenarchi. Irenarchus martyr Sebastensis, ab adolescentia lictoris officium ad multandos sanctos martyres præstabat. Sed cum videret abreptas ad quæstionem a Maximo, Sebastes præfecto, temporibus Diocletiani imperatoris, christianas mulieres multo fortiores esse, quam sexus muliebris ferre posset, editisque miraculis tyrannum confundere; divina Christi gratia illuminatus, sumpto animo Christum aperte confessus, seipsum etiam Christianum prædicavit. Et primum quidem præsidis jussu in lacum injicitur: deinde cum ex eo incolumis evasisset, in ardentem fornacem immittitur; unde etiam sine ullo detrimento egressus, capite plectitur, et spiritus quidem ejus in cœlos evolavit; reliquiæ vero in terrâ sepultæ jacent.
EODEM MENSE. Die vicesima nona 219 Commemoratio sancti Patris nostri Joannis Damasceni. Hic Leone Isauro, ejusque filio Constantino imperatoribus, floruit in urbe Damasco, genere, nobilitate ac fide clarus, patre virtutibus insigni natus; omnemque Græcorum disciplinam, necnon divinas Scripturas assecutus. Ob Christiam autem fidem patriis opibus derelictis, monachus factus est una cum Cosma, cujus plures exstant hymni, qui in sanctis festis, ac præcipue in magna sancti Paschatis hebdomada decantantur. Sanctus vero Joannes multa verborum ubertate sententiarumque gravitate, atque sacræ Scripturæ auctoritate hæresim Iconomachorum confundens, sæpe carcere, sæpe ærumnis ab hæreticis imperatoribus multatus, vexatusque, in optima confessione decessit, et a discipulo suo sepultus fuit.
Eodem die 220 Certamen sancti martyris Philomeni. Philomenus martyr, Aureliano imperatore, ex regione Lycaoniæ, negotia exercens, advehendis frumentis Ancyræ in Galatia operam dabat. Quod autem Christianus esset, apud præfectum Ancyræ delatus, ejus tribunali sistitur. Cumque Christum omnium Deum Dominum profiteretur, primum quidem ferreis clavis pedibus manibusque confixis, in equuleum raptus, scorpionibus laceratur; deinde e ligno depositus, in ardentem fornacem conjicitur: sed nullo, Dei virtute, damno affectus, pedibus iterum, manibus, et capite clavis trajectis, triginta stadia cursu conficere compellitur, donec cursu et labore confectus, beatam animam suam Deo tradidit, pro quo martyrium subiit.


[185] {#pg.117.185}
ΜΙΝΙ ΤΩ ΑΥΤΩ.
"Αθλησις τοῦ ἁγίου καὶ πανευφήμου ἀποστόλου 'Ανδρέου, ἀδελφοῦ τοῦ μεγάλου Πέτρου. Οὗτος, ἀδελφὸς γνήσιος ὑπάρχων τοῦ μεγάλου ἀποστόλου Πέτρου, μετὰ τὴν ἀνάληψιν τοῦ Κυρίου ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, ἐκήρυξεν εἰς πᾶσαν τὴν παράλιον τῆς Βιθυνίας, καὶ τοῦ Πόντου, καὶ τῆς Θράκης, καὶ τῆς Σκυθίας. Μετὰ δὲ ταῦτα ἀπῆλθεν ἐν Σεβαστουπόλει τῇ μεγάλῃ ἐν ᾗ παρεμβάλλουσιν ὁ Ψάρας, καὶ ὁ Φάσις οἱ ποταμοί. Οὗτινος Φάσιος ἐσώτεροι οἱ Αἰθίοπες κατοικοῦσιν. Εἶτα ἀπῆλθεν ἐν Πάτραις τῆς Ἀχαΐας εἰς Πελοπόννησον. Καὶ ἐσταυρώθη ὑπὸ Αἰγεάτου τοῦ ἀνθυπάτου· καὶ οὕτως ἐτελειώθη. Τὸ δὲ ἱερὸν αὐτοῦ λείψανον τότε μὲν ἐτέθη ἐν Πάτραις τῆς Ἀχαΐας· ὕστερον δὲ ἀνακομισθέν, προστάξει τοῦ βασιλέως Κωνσταντίνου, παρὰ τοῦ ἁγίου Ἀρτεμίου ἐν Κωνσταντινουπόλει κατετέθη, μετὰ τῶν ἁγίων ἀποστόλων Λουκᾶ καὶ Τιμοθέου, ὑπὸ τὴν ἱερὰν τράπεζαν τῶν ἁγίων ἀποστόλων, βρύον ἰαμάτων χάριν.


[186] {#pg.117.186}
NOVEMBER.
EODEM MENSE. Die tricesima 22 Certamen sancti et clarissimi apostoli Andreæ, fratris magni Petri. Hic germanus magni apostoli Petri frater, post ascensionem Domini nostri Jesu Christi, prædicavit in tota maritima ora Bithyniæ, Ponti, Thraciæ, et Scythiæ: deinde magnam Sebastopolim lustravit, quam fluvii duo interscindunt, Psarus, et Phasis, cujus sunt accolæ inferiores Æthiopes. Postremo Patras Achaiæ in Peloponnesum migravit, ubi ab Ægea proconsule in crucem sublatus est: atque ita mortem obiit. Sacræ vero ejus reliquiæ tunc quidem Patris in Achaia depositæ; postea vero Constantini imperatoris jussu a sancto Artemio, una cum sanctis apostolis Luca et Timotheo, Constantinopolim translatæ, sub sacra apostolorum mensa conditæ fuerunt, gratiam curationum emittentes.
MENOLOGII GRÆCORUM PARS SECUNDA MENSE DECEMBRI AD FEBRUARIUM.
MHNI ΔΕΚΕΜΒΡΙΩ
Ἐν τῇ πρώτῃ. Μνήμη τοῦ ἁγίου, καὶ ἐνδόξου προφήτου Ναούμ. Ὁ μέγας καὶ ἔνδοξος προφήτης Ναοὺμ ἐγένετο ἀπὸ τῆς φυλῆς Συμεὼν καὶ, τῆς χώρας τῶν Ἰουδαίων. Εὐλαβὴς δὲ ὢν, ἐδέξατο παρὰ Θεοῦ προφητείας χάρισμα· καὶ προεφήτευσε κατὰ Νινευὴ τῆς πόλεως, μετὰ τὸ προφητεῦσαι κατ᾿ αὐτῆς πρότερον τὸν Ἰωνᾶν. Καὶ εἶπεν, ὅτι Μέλλει ἡ Νινευὴ ὑπὸ ὑδάτων γλυκέων καὶ πυρὸς ἀφανισθῆναι. "Ο καὶ γέγονε· τῇ γὰρ κελεύσει τοῦ Θεοῦ, διὰ τὰς ἁμαρτίας αὐτῶν ἡ περιέχουσα τὴν Νινευὴ λίμνη, σεισμοῦ γενομένου, κατεπόντισεν αὐτήν. Καὶ πῦρ ἐκ τῆς ἐρήμου ἐπελθὸν, τὰ ἐναπομεῖναν μέρος αὐτῆς κατέκαυσε. Καὶ μετὰ τὸ προφητεῦσαι πάντα ταῦτα, ἀπέθανε, καὶ ὑπὸ τῶν συγγενῶν αὐτοῦ ἐτάφη ἐν λείψανον αὐτοῦ ἐντίμως ἐν τῇ γῇ αὐτοῦ. Ἦν δὲ ὅτε ἐτελεύτησεν, ἐτῶν τεσσαράκοντα καὶ πέντε. Εἶχε δὲ γένειον μὲν στρογγύλον· ἰσχνὸν πρόσωπον, ἁπλᾶς τρίχας. Ἦν δὲ καὶ ὑποφάλαικρος, καὶ τὴν κεφαλὴν ἔχων ἐπιμήκη.
Τῇ αὐτῇ ἡμέρᾳ "Αθλησις τοῦ ἁγίου μάρτυρος Ἀνανίου. Ἀνανίας ὁ μάρτυς ἐγένετο μὲν ἀπὸ τῆς χώρας
MEMSE DECEMBRI. Die prima 1 Commemoratio sancti et gloriosi prophetæ Nahum. Magnus et gloriosus propheta Nahum de tribu Simeon, ex regione Judææ, fuit, vir singulari in Deum pietate. Cum autem prophetiæ donum a Deo accepisset, prophetavit adversus Niniven urbem, post Jonam prophetam, dicens: Ninive dulcibus aquis ignique subvertetur. Quod et contigit: divino enim jussu propter eorum peccata stagnum, quod urbem ipsam ambibat, terræmotu facto, illam submersit, et ignis ab eremo irruens reliquias ejus penitus absumpsit. Postquam autem hæc omnia prædixisset, decessit, ejusque corpus a propinquis in terra sua honorifice conditum fuit. Cum vero obiit, annos quadraginta quinque agebat. Erat autem mento rotundo, facie gracili, tenui capillo, capite subcalvo ac procero.
Eodem die 3 Certamen sancti martyris Ananiæ. Ananias martyr ex Persidis regione fuit, laicus
surrounding crowd and embraced her brother, and struggled along with him; whereupon they were thrown into a boiling cauldron and came out unharmed. Seeing this miracle, two hundred soldiers believed in Christ and were beheaded along with them.
On the same day.
Commemoration of our holy Father Bassianus.
Our holy Father Bassianus was from the eastern region of the Syrians. In the time of the pious Emperor Marcian, he came to Constantinople. And the Emperor, having recognized his virtue, honored him as a father to such a degree that he even built a church in his name, which exists to this day. He was, moreover, so adept at instruction and teaching that he persuaded many to leave the world and become monks. Among whom was the holy Matrona. For the number of his disciples grew to three hundred. And he also performed many miracles. Furthermore, if anyone had fallen into some very grave sin and for that reason was despairing of his salvation, the holy man, having taken upon himself a grief equal to the sinner’s, would not cease in his effort until he had snatched the fallen one from the hands of the devil and brought him to the most merciful God through repentance. Moreover, being illustrious with the gift of prophecy, by predicting the future he benefited many. Having lived his life in this manner, he departed to the Lord in extreme old age, and was buried by his disciples in the aforementioned church of his.
On the same day.
Commemoration of our holy Father Jacob the Ascetic.
Our holy Father Jacob became great among ascetics, having often gained victory over the devil, and although at one time he was defeated and fell, yet again he engaged in conflict with him and emerged superior in the battle. For having lived in a cave and remained in stillness for fifteen years, he received from God the power to drive out demons. But the devil, moved by envy, wanting to cast him into fornication and murder, entered into the daughter of a certain rich man and caused her to call upon the holy man. Therefore, the girl’s parents, searching and finding the cave, brought the daughter to him and begged him to heal her; and he, having prayed, drove the demon out of the girl. And her parents, fearing that the demon might enter into her again, left her there until she should be completely healed. The ascetic then defiled the girl; afterwards he murdered her; and being afraid, he fled. And on the way, having repented and being poured out in tears, he hid himself in a tomb; where he expiated for his sin so thoroughly that, in a great drought, he obtained rain from heaven through his prayer; and thus he reached his end.
IN THE SAME MONTH.
The eleventh day.
Commemoration of the holy sisters Zenaides and Philonilla.
Holy Zenais, with her sister Philonilla, was from the city of Tarsus, being related by blood to the holy apostle Paul. And at first, indeed, she practiced the medical art. But having been taught by him, she left behind both the art...
and all her wealth, and her sister. And she went up to the mountain, and finding a cave, she entered into it, and lived in quiet, attending only to God and to herself, and being nourished by the herbs growing near the cave. One day, having gone out of the cave to gather herbs, she trod upon a very sharp wooden nail, fixed in the ground by the malice of the devil, who bore most indignantly the fact that many, coming to her secretly, were being converted to the faith of Christ. And when she sat down, unable to bear the pain, to remove the nail from her foot, she yielded up her spirit to the Lord. Philonilla, meanwhile, remaining at home—having striven for virtue with no less zeal than her sister, and constantly following God with great devotion—fell asleep in peace.
Eodem die
Commemoration of the holy apostle Philip, of whom mention is made in the Acts of the holy apostles.
Philip, the apostle of Christ, was one of the seven deacons. And while he was in the city of Joppa preaching Christ, by the command of the Holy Spirit, as he was traveling from Joppa toward the city of Gaza, he met the minister of the queen of the Ethiopians, being himself a eunuch, named Candace, seated on a chariot and reading the prophet Isaiah. And approaching him, he asked him: "Do you understand what you are reading?" And he said: "How could I, unless someone guides me?" And immediately he brought him up onto the chariot, and explained to him what he was reading, and led him to the faith of Christ. And finding water, the eunuch says to Philip: "Behold water, what prevents me from being baptized?" And descending from the chariot, he was baptized. And he saw Philip no more, for he was snatched away, but continued his journey rejoicing. Philip, however, having become bishop in Tralles of Asia, ceased to live.
EODEM MENSE.
Die duodecima.
Commemoration of the holy seventh synod of the three hundred and sixty-seven holy Fathers.
The holy, great, and ecumenical seventh synod was held at Nicaea for the second time, under the reign of Constantine and his mother Irene; the most holy and God-bearing Father, Tarasius, being Patriarch of Constantinople. The holy Fathers, being three hundred and sixty-seven in number, assembled by the command of the emperor against the Iconoclasts. And they anathematized in writing every heresy, and the leaders of the heresies, and then also the Iconoclasts themselves; all with one voice exclaiming: "Whoever does not venerate the holy images is a stranger to the faith of the orthodox; and the honor that is rendered to the image passes to the prototype," as the great Basil said: "whoever adores and honors the image, adores and honors in it the hypostasis of the One whom the image represents." When they had decreed these things, and the orthodox faith
had been confirmed in writing, and they had been honored by the emperor, each returned to his own bishopric.
On the same day: The Contest of the Holy Martyrs Probus, Tarachus, and Andronicus
The three celebrated martyrs, Probus, Tarachus, and Andronicus, lived during the reign of Diocletian. Being Christians and confessing Christ, they were seized by the prefect of Cilicia. First, Tarachus, having had his face struck, was shut up in prison. Secondly, Probus was beaten and, having been fettered in iron chains, was also delivered to the prison. And the third, the holy Andronicus, having been beaten, having had his thighs slashed with a sword, and his sides flayed, and having been rubbed with salt, was cast with the others into the same prison. Thereafter, having been bound, they were led from Cilicia to Mopsuestia. And because they could not be persuaded to deny Christ, they were tortured again. Tarachus had his mouth crushed and his head shaved. Andronicus was beaten on the belly with rods. And Probus was burned in his armpits and loins with glowing iron skewers. And having endured more torments than these, they were finally beheaded.
On the same day: The Contest of the Holy Martyr Anastasia the Virgin
The martyr of Christ, Anastasia, lived during the reign of Decius and Valerian, and was from the city of Rome. She was a monastic, living with other virgins in a certain monastery. She was therefore denounced to the prefect. He, sending for her, brought her before him in a neck-iron; and when she stood before the prefect and was questioned, she confessed the name of Christ. She was struck on the face and stripped; and she was burned by fire, with pitch, oil, and sulfur poured over her. Then she was hung upon a stake; and as the instrument was turned, her bones cracked. Then her two breasts were cut off, and her nails were torn out. And after these things, her two hands were cut off. Then her two feet; and her teeth were rooted out; and having endured many other tortures, finally she received her end by the sword. And thus she delivered her spirit to the Lord.
On the same day: The Contest of the Holy Martyr Domnina
The martyr of Christ, Domnina, lived during the reign of the emperor Diocletian and was from the city of Anazarbus; she was seized as a Christian by the Greek executioners. She was brought to the judgment seat of Lysias the duke. And having confessed with boldness before all the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, and declaring Him to be the true God and Creator of all, and demonstrating the gods of the Greeks to be false—namely, stones and wood and rotten material—she was most cruelly beaten, and her feet were burned with glowing skewers:
then she is cast into prison. Being brought again to a second trial, she is beaten severely with rods, and her joints are disjointed by means of tongs. And she is again cast into prison. And being in it, giving thanks and praying, she commended her blessed soul to God, for whom she also suffered martyrdom. And her body was honorably buried.
OF THE SAME MONTH. THE THIRTEENTH DAY.
B 116 The Contest of the holy martyrs Carpus, Papylus, and those with them, Agathodorus and Agathonica.
The holy martyrs Carpus and Papylus flourished under the emperor Decius. Now the holy Carpus was bishop of Thyatira; but Papylus was a deacon. Being therefore apprehended by the prefect of Asia, and questioned, they confessed before all the name of the Lord Christ. And being forced to sacrifice to idols, and not being persuaded, they were bound to horses and dragged before the prefect as far as the city of Sardis, and there being put upon the rack, he ordered them to be flayed. But Agathodorus, who was the servant of the saints and followed them, having been tortured, died. But the holy Carpus, while hanging on the rack, smiled; and when the prefect asked for what reason he did this, Carpus replied: "I beheld the glory of God, and I rejoiced." Being taken down from the racks, they were thrown into the fire. And the sister of Papylus, Agathonica, who was then present, entered the furnace with joy of her own accord; and both gave up their souls to the Lord.
ON THE SAME DAY.
The Contest of the holy martyr Florentius.
The martyr Florentius was from the city of Thessalonica. Being a Christian and very zealous for the good, he heaped reproaches and slanders upon the gods of the Greeks before everyone, and anathematized their superstition; but he taught all the true faith of Christ, and strengthened the Christians, and exhorted them to fulfill the will of God. For which cause he was apprehended by the prefect of the region, and being questioned, he confessed before him that our Lord Jesus Christ is the sole God, but that the gods of the Greeks are wood, and stones, and gold, and silver, and bronze, and iron, idols without soul or sense. When he had said these things, having provoked the prefect to anger, he is tortured in various ways. Then he is hung upon a beam, and flayed. Finally, a great pyre having been kindled, he is thrown into it with joy. And in it, praying and giving thanks to God, he was perfected.
OF THE SAME MONTH. THE FOURTEENTH DAY.
118 The Contest of the holy martyrs Nazarius, Gervasius, Protasius, and Celsus.
These saints were famous at Rome, while Nero was emperor.
Nazarius was the son of Africanus and Perpetua, who were baptized by Saint Peter; he himself was baptized by Linus, the patriarch of Rome. And when he was twenty years old, he traveled through the cities of Italy, preaching Christ. Then he came to a certain city where he found Gervasius and Protasius, who had been cast into prison by the governor of the region on account of their faith in Christ. Being beaten and expelled from the city, he went to another city and took with him Celsus, a boy of three years. Then, reaching the city of Milan, he found the holy Gervasius and Protasius in prison again. And being seized, he was sent to Rome by the magistrate. And having returned again to Milan, he was beheaded, along with the other saints and the boy Celsus, who was then nine years old and seven months.
OF THE SAME MONTH. THE FIFTEENTH DAY.
The Contest of the holy martyr Lucian
Lucian the martyr was from Antioch of Syria, in the reign of Diocletian. After the death of his parents, having given his possessions to the poor, he gave himself entirely to the reading of the divine Scriptures. And he turned many of the Greeks to the Lord. Leaving Antioch, he reached Nicomedia, where Diocletian was killing the Christians. And because he found many who, for fear of the torments, wanted to deny the Christian faith, he gave them courage and persuaded them to suffer martyrdom. He became so similar to God in virtue that, as he went about the city, he could be seen by those he wished, and by those he did not wish, he could not be seen at all. Diocletian, learning of this and fearing to bring him into his presence lest he be rebuked by him, hung a veil and met with him. When he could move his mind from his purpose by no art, he ordered him to be starved to death. And so, having tasted no food for many days, he finished his course in prison. And he was thrown into the sea.
On the same day
Commemoration of our holy Father Sabinus the bishop
The blessed Sabinus, on account of his surpassing virtue and character, was first ordained bishop. But later, disdaining the tumults of the world for the sake of the beauty of stillness, he withdrew, fleeing into the deserts; and he strove so much toward virtue, giving himself over to fasts, and vigils, and ascetic labors, that he was found worthy of many miracles: to drive away diseases, to cleanse lepers, to cast out demons, and to foretell the future. Having benefited many, and having caused them to advance toward virtue through his teaching—for many came to him for the sake of benefit, and he persuaded them to leave behind parents, and brothers, and kinsmen, and to approach the monastic life, and to serve Christ, and to attend to Him through purity and the guarding of the commandments
committing his holy soul to the Lord, and his precious relics to the kindred earth, which even until this day pour forth healings.
IN THE SAME MONTH.
The sixteenth day.
The contest of the holy martyr Longinus the centurion.
Under Tiberius Caesar, there was a centurion from the region of Cappadocia, serving under Pilate, the governor of Judea, by whose command he ministered to the precious passion and cross of Christ together with his century. But when he had seen the miracles that took place when Christ was raised upon the cross—namely, the earthquake, the failing of the sun, the graves opened, the dead raised to life, the rocks rent asunder—he cried out: Truly this was the Son of God. And having renounced the duty of the military service subject to him, he returned to his own country, preaching Christ. Having learned of this, the Jews bribed Pilate with money, who wrote to Tiberius against Longinus. And he, having sent word, beheaded him and the two soldiers with him, ordering his head to be brought to Jerusalem; which was cast into a dung-heap, where it lay hidden for a long time, until a certain blind woman from Cappadocia, admonished by a divine vision, set out for Jerusalem, found the head, and received her sight.
IN THE SAME MONTH.
The seventeenth day.
The contest of the holy martyrs Chrysanthus and Daria. 17
These flourished under the emperor Numerian; and Chrysanthus, who had set out for Rome with his own father, a man of the senatorial order, was instructed in the faith of Christ by a certain bishop hiding in a cave. When his father learned of this, he shut him up in a dark prison; then he compelled him to take a wife, Daria, who was imbued with philosophical studies. Yet he treated her no otherwise than as a sister, since both had adopted the plan that they would preserve their virginity until death. Wherefore she too was baptized. But when both began to induce the people to live chastely and temperately, the unbelievers, bearing this very ill because their wives, having divorced them, were betrothed to Christ, reported the matter to the prefect, who delivered them to the tribune Claudius. Being led therefore out of the city, and tortured with various torments, they were cast into a pit full of mire; and being covered up and trampled, they died.
On the same day.
Commemoration of the holy prophet Osee.
The prophet Osee was from the region of Judea, worshipping the God of heaven and earth, and hating and execrating the gods of the nations. And correcting the errors of his own fellow-tribesmen the Jews, he received a maximum gift of prophecy: and he prophesied much indeed against Israel, and against the nations; but he gave this chief sign, that the Lord would come, and in
earth would converse with men. He gave another sign: The sun, he said, shall set in Siloam; and when the tree in Siloam is divided into twelve parts, twelve trees shall be born. That is: when the Jews crucify Christ, the Sun of righteousness, and He dies and sets, and hides His rays, the twelve tribes of Israel shall be divided into all the nations, and there shall arise twelve trees, which are the twelve apostles, and they shall teach all nations to believe in Christ. Having prophesied these things, he departed in peace and was buried.
On the same day
The Contest of the Holy Anargyroi Cosmas and Damian, and their brothers Anthimus, Leontius, and Euprepius
There are three pairs of holy Anargyroi, who were called by the same names of Cosmas and Damian, and who practiced the art of medicine, and had the surname of Anargyroi. For some were sons of the most pious woman Theodote, and having died in peace, they were buried in a place called Phereman. Others resided in Rome, having an envious teacher, who, leading them by deceit into a mountain as if to gather herbs, murdered them with stones. But the others, whose contest we are about to narrate, were Arabs, who, wandering through the world, healed the sick freely and without reward. But in the times of Diocletian, having traveled into the region of Cilicia, they were seized and brought before Lysias the duke; who, having tortured them in various ways, afterwards beheaded them, along with their three following brothers, Anthimus, Leontius, and Euprepius.
On the same month, the 18th
The Commemoration of the holy apostle and evangelist Luke.
Luke the evangelist was from the great city of Antioch, a physician by profession, and a painter. Having gone to Thebes of Macedonia in the time of Tiberius the emperor, because of the persecution, he met the holy apostle Paul; and as he was one of the seventy apostles, he joined himself as a companion to the same apostle, and preached Christ with him. And he cooperated with a willing mind in the ministry of the preaching and the Gospel. He wrote first the holy Gospel which bears his name, to a certain prefect, Theophilus, who had believed in Christ, while the holy apostle Peter dictated to him; then he set forth the Acts of the apostles to the same Theophilus. And having done these things, he gave up his soul in peace to God. His holy and precious relics were buried first in Thebes; but afterwards, having been brought from Thebes to Constantinople, they were deposited in the church of the Holy Apostles.
On the same month, the 19th
The Contest of the holy martyr Sadoth, and the one hundred and twenty saints with him.
Sadoth the martyr flourished under Sapor king of the Persians; because of his faith in Christ he was seized and brought to him; and not being persuaded...
to worship the fire, he is beaten mercilessly. His skin is flayed from his forehead down to his feet, and a thong a finger’s breadth wide is stripped from his hide. Again he is beaten with oxhide thongs. And he is dragged naked over thorns, wood, and very sharp stones. Then his fingers are cut off joint by joint, and he is thrust into a screw-press and squeezed. After these things, his remaining body is pierced with red-hot skewers, and then, hung head downward, his tongue is cut out. A thousand two hundred and seventy men, seeing his steadfastness, and considering that if he did not have great hopes he would not have endured such things, believed in Christ. And they were delivered to prison. He himself, giving thanks to God, was beheaded along with one hundred and twenty other saints.
On the same day
Commemoration of our holy Father Amphilochius, bishop of Iconium.
This man was ordained bishop of Iconium, not by the hands of men, but by the hands of holy angels. Being a fellow-soldier of the holy Fathers Basil the Great, Gregory the Theologian, and Gregory of Nyssa, he stood in battle against the heretics, Arius and Eunomius and their like. His life extended until the time of Theodosius the Great, by whom he was chosen to curb the assemblies of the Arians in the cities. The emperor, however, deeming this burdensome because of the turmoil it might cause, at that time refrained. But later, having come to the emperor Theodosius for a certain necessity, and having paid obeisance only to him, while passing over his son Arcadius who was sitting nearby, when he was asked why he did so, he replied: "Just as you, O emperor, take it ill if equal honor is not paid to your son as to you, so too know that injury is done to God the Father if anyone treats the Son and Word with contumely." By these words he moved the emperor so much that he brought the matter to a conclusion according to his judgment and will. Having lived his life with piety in this manner, he departed to the Lord.
On the same day
Commemoration of the holy and glorious prophet Joel.
He was from the region of Judea. Following the will of the Lord, he received the gift of prophecy. He prophesied many other things, but far more about Christ, namely that He was to be born of a Virgin. He also prophesied concerning the Holy Spirit, which the holy twelve apostles received on the great feast of Pentecost, saying: "Thus saith the Lord: And it shall come to pass in the last days, I will pour out of my Spirit upon all flesh; and your sons and your daughters shall prophesy." And this is what came to pass. For when the holy apostles were sitting in the upper room on the day of Pentecost, at the third hour of the day, the Holy Spirit descended in the form of fiery tongues, and sat upon each one of them, and gave them various gifts:
by his words, he was delivered to the sword. But to them, not only to those who were then, but also to those who succeeded them, he distributed diverse gifts and prophecies. And having prophesied these things, he fell asleep in peace and was buried in his own land.
IN THE SAME MONTH, THE 20TH DAY
Commemoration of the holy Cornelius the centurion.
Cornelius the centurion was from Caesarea of Palestine, a commander of a hundred soldiers in the times of the holy apostles; he was the first of the Gentiles to believe in Christ. For God, seeing his alms which he did for the poor, accepted him and through an angel admonished him to be baptized by holy Peter. Then he was ordained bishop of Scamander. While he was preaching Christ there, he was seized by the governor Demetrius. And he was compelled to enter into the temple of idols and to sacrifice together with the people, his wife, and his son. But when he had gone and prayed to God, there was an earthquake: the idols fell, and the temple as well; it covered the son of the governor and his wife. As they cried out from beneath, saying that the God of Cornelius is great, the governor besought him, and he prayed; and they came out. And the governor, along with his wife and son and two hundred and seventy-seven men, believed; and thus he finished his life in peace.
ON THE SAME DAY
The contest of the holy martyr Artemius the wonderworker.
Artemius, glorious in many contests, flourished under the reign of Julian the Apostate; he was the dux of Alexandria in Egypt, having been among the patricians distinguished by the Emperor Constantine the Great. When he heard that Julian was punishing the Christians at Antioch, he went to him, reproving his lawlessness. He was beaten by him with ox-hide scourges; his loins were lacerated with sharp thorns, and his sides and eyelids pierced with hooks. He was shut inside a stone that had been cut in two by a stone-cutter. And when the parts were fitted back together and compressed, he endured intolerable tortures: his eyes burst forth and his entrails were spilled out. Since he could not be turned from his pious purpose by the words of the tyrant, he met his end—beheaded by the sword. His holy relics were translated to Constantinople and deposited there, restoring health to those who approach him with faith.
IN THE SAME MONTH, THE 21ST DAY
The contest of the holy martyrs Dasius, Gaius, and Zoticus.
These holy martyrs flourished in the city of Nicomedia. When a public festival for the idols was being held, speaking with boldness, they overturned the things that were placed upon the altars; and having acted piously, they were considered by the foolish, ungodly people to have acted impiously. Wherefore, having been thrown into chains, they were dragged to the judge.
they were brought before the judge. He applied every sort of torture to them: indeed, he scourged them mercilessly, hung them on the rack, and inflicted wounds; he tore their sides with whips and lacerated their flesh with haircloth garments. Since they not only despised all these things but also rebuked the vanity of the idols and with a loud voice testified that Christ alone is the true God, he hung great stones about their necks and cast them into the depths of the sea. There they reached the end of their martyrdom, rendering thanks to Christ, the King of the ages, unto death, that they had been found worthy to die for Him.
Eodem die
Commemoratio sancti Patris nostri Hilarionis, presbyteri et anachoretæ.
Saint Hilarion was born in Gaza of Palestine and flourished under the Emperor Constantine the Great. Having set out for Alexandria in Egypt for the sake of his studies, by the grace of God he obtained two things: that he should learn both literature and those things which pertain to the faith of Christ. Therefore, having been baptized and having learned the discipline and instruction of the great Antony, he went to him and, having become his disciple, he learned the works of virtue from him for some years. Soon having returned to his homeland, when his parents had already departed this life, he distributed all his property to the poor. Having donned a hairshirt and a sheepskin, he sought the desert, where, leading a solitary life, he received the grace of miracles. However, wishing to avoid the company of men for the love of quiet, he often changed his place, now here, now there. And when he had lived eighty years, of which he had spent seventy in monastic discipline, he migrated to the Lord.
EODEM MENSE
Die vicesima secunda
Commemoratio sancti et apostolorum æqualis Patris nostri Abercii, episcopi Hierapolis.
Our holy Father Abercius flourished under the Emperor Marcus, having been ordained bishop of Hierapolis by the holy apostles; he was a most famous worker of miracles. But the demon, wishing to tempt him, entered into the emperor's daughter and caused her to seek the saint so that she might be healed by him. Summoned therefore by imperial letters, he came to Rome. He had commanded his servant to pour vinegar, garum, and oil into one and the same skin, from which, if the saint ordered anything to be drawn for himself, they flowed out separately and individually; but if the servant dared to take any for himself stealthily, they flowed out mixed together. Therefore, when he had arrived in Rome, the emperor's daughter was freed, and he commanded the demon to carry a stone altar, which lay in his sight, to Hierapolis. "You," he said, "were the author of so great a journey for me to Rome; I, however, command you to take up the stone and place it over my tomb." Which also
came to pass. And having lived the rest of his life in holiness, he departed to the Lord.
Eodem die
The Suffering of the holy Hieromartyr Alexander, and those with him: Heraclius, Anna, Theodota, and Glyceria
Alexander, being a holy martyr and bishop, and preaching Christ with boldness, and by his teaching converting many from both the Jews and the Greeks to the faith and baptizing them, was seized by the governor of the region. And having been punished in many ways, and scraped, and compelled to sacrifice to the idols, he was not persuaded. But a certain soldier, Heraclius by name, seeing his steadfastness, believed in Christ; wherefore he too was punished with many torments and was beheaded. And after this the holy Alexander performed miracles—for he was healed of his wounds by the visitation of Christ—and he drew three women to the faith of the Christians: Anna, Theodota, and Glyceria. When they stood before the governor, they denounced him and called the worship of idols false. Then the governor, becoming enraged, first beheaded the holy Alexander, and then, after torturing the holy women, he cut off their heads, and thus they were perfected.
OF THE SAME MONTH. 23rd day.
The Suffering of the holy Hieromartyr and Apostle James, the Brother of the Lord
The holy James was the first bishop in Jerusalem, having been ordained by the Lord Himself. And he was the first to write the divine Liturgy, having been taught it by Christ. But later, Basil the Great, and after him Chrysostom, rendered it more concise on account of the infirmity of men. Now the holy James, by teaching many of the Jews and Greeks and converting them to the Lord, stirred the Jews to anger. They gathered together, seized him, cast him down from the pinnacle of the Temple, and killed him. Regarding why he is called the "Brother of God," such a tale is brought down by tradition: When the betrothed Joseph was dividing his land among his sons from his first wife, and wished to allot a portion to the Son and God of the holy Virgin, the others indeed did not agree; but James, taking Him into his own portion as a co-heir, was called not only the Brother of God, but also the Just.
Eodem die
The Suffering of the holy martyrs Theodota and Socrates the Priest
Saint Theodota lived under the Emperor Alexander; she was born of noble parents and was from the region of Pontus. When she had gone away into a certain monastery in search of quiet, she was discovered. And having been seized by the governor for the sake of Christ, she was twice suspended and scraped; and into a furnace heated...
with fire she was cast. But by the grace of Christ, she was saved from this and brought by the governor to Byzantium. Then in Ancyra, where the presbyter Socrates was celebrating the public festival of the Greeks for their demons and bowing down to their idols, he, being pious and confessing our Lord Jesus Christ, was filled with zeal and overturned the altars of the sacrifices, for which reason he was beheaded and thus delivered up his spirit. And Saint Theodota, having been compelled to sacrifice to the idols and not having been persuaded, was cast into a cauldron of boiling oil. And by the grace of God, having come out of the cauldron unharmed, she too was perfected by the sword.
Eodem die
137
Commemoration of the manifestation of the seven holy youths of Ephesus.
These saints, Maximilian, Iamblichus, Martinianus, Dionysius, John, Constantine, and Antoninus, flourished at Ephesus under the emperor Decius. Being seized for their confession of Christ and brought before the emperor, when they asked for time to deliberate and obtained it, they fled to a certain mountain and hid themselves in a cave, and there fell asleep, the entrance to the cave having been blocked. And after three hundred and seventy-two years had passed, under the reign of Theodosius the Younger, when heretics had introduced a doctrine denying the resurrection, and this matter was causing the emperor the greatest anxiety, God, in order to confirm in the minds of all the sure faith of the resurrection, willed to awaken them alive by opening the cave. From among them, Iamblichus, having taken a coin from the time of Decius and descended into the city to buy food, as one who had fallen asleep only yesterday, was recognized and brought- C -to the bishop, and explained the whole matter as it had occurred. Therefore, having borne witness to the resurrection, they died again and were carried into the cave.
Eodem die
138
Commemoration of our holy Father Ignatius, Patriarch of Constantinople.
Our holy Father Ignatius was the son of the emperor Michael, brother of Theophilus, and grandson of the emperors Nicephorus. Having become a eunuch and then a monk, he also became hegumen of the monastery of the Archangel, which then was called that of the Orient, but now goes by the name of Satyros. Afterwards, he was created Patriarch of Constantinople, and when he had presided for eleven years and five months, he was driven out by the emperor Michael, who appointed in his place Photius, who had previously been made a monk. But he himself was afterwards- D -ejected by the emperor Basil, and Ignatius was restored to his see, which, when he had occupied it for eleven years, he was again deposed, and holy Stephen, son of the emperor Basil, was substituted for him. But Saint Ignatius, withdrawing to the monastery of Satyros, after living a pious and religious life, passed away in peace.
EODEM MENSE.
Die vicesima quarta
139
Contest of the holy martyr Arethas and his companions.
This man was prince of the city of Negra in Ethiopia,
during the reign of Justin, when Elesbaan, a most Christian man, was king of Ethiopia. As for the Homerites, their region is called Saba in Holy Scripture, but Arabia Felix by the Greeks; and a certain Hebrew ruled over them. Elesbaan, however, having overcome the Hebrew, had fortified his city with garrisons. But the Hebrew, having suddenly slaughtered the garrison, marched against the city of Negra, which he captured, not by force, but by perjuries; and he slaughtered all the Christians found there, both men and women. There lay the holy Arethas, who was an old man and unable to walk; and because they were attempting to behead him, he was carried by others to the slaughter. Being brought there, he was beheaded, giving thanks to God, together with the rest of the Christians found in the city of Negra; and thus he received the end of his life.
On the same day
Commemoration of our holy Father Proclus, Patriarch of Constantinople.
Our holy Father Proclus, being pious, wise, and virtuous, was ordained bishop of Cyzicus by the holy Sisinius, Patriarch of Constantinople. But, having departed there and not being received by the clergy—who were heretics—he returned to Constantinople, where he passed the intervening time in leisure, devoting himself to reading. When Patriarch Maximianus died, and while his remains were still lying in the sanctuary of the most holy Great Church of God, he was chosen as Patriarch of Constantinople; and he was enthroned during that very Great Week, on the Holy Thursday of the saving passion of our Lord Jesus Christ. Having conducted himself honorably after his ordination and having benefited many through his teaching, he oversaw the Church for twelve years and three months, and having reached his perfection, he passed away.
IN THE SAME MONTH. 25th
Contest of the holy martyrs and notaries Marcian and Martyrius.
These men were students and notaries of the holy Paul the Confessor, who had been Patriarch of Constantinople during the reign of Constantius. Since, therefore, the holy Paul had been exiled to Armenia by that same emperor because he would not accept the heresy of the Arians, nor communion with them, but rather renounced them—where he also received a blessed end, being strangled by the Arians for the sake of his orthodox confession—these men, holding the same views as their teacher, reproved the Arians. They were seized by Philip the Prefect, who was an Arian, and after being tortured in many ways, were shut in a dark prison, being tormented by hunger and darkness. Being brought out from the prison, they were compelled to join the heresy of the Arians; but as they were not persuaded, they were taken to the summit of the wall and slaughtered with swords, where their remains were also interred.
On the same day 142 The Contest of the holy martyr Varus and the seven saints with him.
The holy martyr Varus lived under the Emperor Maximian, serving in Egypt, and was of distinguished lineage and piety. When certain holy men, who had previously spent much time in mountains and caves, were captured and handed over to prison for the sake of Christ, the holy Varus entered the prison. He ministered to the saints with great kindness, relieving them of their chains, and they were the ones who urged him to speak boldly and to worship Christ openly and publicly, rather than in secret. Therefore, when one of the seven died in the same prison, the holy Varus took his place. When the prefect learned of this, he had Varus brought before him together with the others and ordered that he confess Christ publicly, be beaten with virgin thongs, and hung up and torn by his sides. In the same way, the six other saints were taken up, tortured with iron claws, and beaten with thongs for five hours until they delivered their spirits to the Lord.
OF THE SAME MONTH. The twenty-sixth day. 143 The Contest of the great holy martyr Demetrios.
The most glorious martyr of Christ, Demetrios, was under the Emperor Maximian, from the city of Thessalonica, a pious man and a teacher of the Christian faith. When Maximian came to Thessalonica, he ordered that the holy Demetrios, who had been accused of the Christian religion, be seized and cast into prison. The same Maximian had a certain gladiator named Lyaeus, of whom he boasted, saying that there was no one who, in single combat with him, would come away the victor. When a certain youth named Nestor learned of this, he went to the prison to the holy Demetrios and received a blessing from him. And having conquered Lyaeus, he killed him. Maximian was grieved by the death of the gladiator; then, learning who was responsible for the death—that it was Demetrios—he ordered him to be put to death in the prison. Soldiers went into the prison and pierced him with spears, and there, lying on the ground, he gave up his spirit. A B
On the same day 144 The Contest of the holy martyrs Mark, Soterichos, and Valentine.
These holy martyrs, having first passed their lives in all piety, preached Christ to all, and brought many of the infidels to the Lord and baptized them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit. Therefore, they were seized by the impious Greeks, tortured with many torments, and their bodies were pierced with long nails. And so, dragged along, pulled, and pitilessly crushed upon the ground by men, women, and children, they delivered their holy and blessed souls to God. But a certain Christian, Terentianus by name, a pious and faithful man,
who was accustomed to encourage the living martyrs to undergo martyrdom, and to show love and honor to the deceased, took the precious relics of the saints from Asia, transported them to the island of Thasos, and deposited them honorably, where they pour out cures to the faithful to this day.
On the same day: The Contest of the Holy Martyr Nestor.
Nestor the martyr flourished in the city of Thessalonica during the reign of the emperor Maximian. Being a Christian, he frequently sought out Saint Demetrios, wherever he was staying, and was instructed by him more accurately in the word of truth. When Maximian happened to be in Thessalonica and was hosting the games, and had brought forth into the theater a certain pagan man, Lyæus by name, a gladiator of monstrous size and strength, who was killing all those who dared to enter into a contest with him, the holy Nestor, having seen this, was filled with divine zeal. And when he heard Maximian saying, "If anyone wishes, let him fight and defeat Lyæus," he ran to the saint and deliberated with him concerning this. Having been strengthened by him and empowered by the strength of our Lord Jesus Christ, he killed Lyæus with a sword. The emperor, being moved to great anger by this, beheaded Nestor. And thus he was perfected, triumphing at once over the devil and over Lyæus.
On the same day: Commemoration of the Great Earthquake.
In the twenty-fourth year of the reign of Leo the Isaurian, during the ninth indiction, in the month of October, on the twenty-sixth day, on the feast of Saint Demetrios, a great and terrible earthquake occurred in Constantinople, so that all the houses and churches collapsed, and an innumerable multitude of mortals perished, crushed in the fall. Hence, celebrating the remembrance of such a terrible earthquake, we hold processions and go to the great and sacred temple of the all-immaculate and most glorious Lady our Mother of God and Ever-Virgin Mary, which is in Blachernae, supplicating and entreating her fervently; and we beseech the Lord our Jesus Christ, who was born of her without seed and without corruption for us sinners, to deliver us from His just wrath stirred up against us. Ever since that time, mindful of such a tribulation, we celebrate this present feast annually with thanksgiving, praying not to fall into such a scourge.
IN THE SAME MONTH. The Twenty-Seventh Day. The Contest of the Holy Martyr Capitolina and her handmaid Eroteis.
The martyr Capitolina lived during the reign of the emperor Diocletian; she was from the region of Cappadocia and very noble. Having been arrested and brought before the governor, she was asked for her name, her city, and her parents. "I,"
she replied, "am called a Christian; my city is the Jerusalem above; and my parents are the teachers of the Christians, and first and foremost the great Firmilianus, bishop of Caesarea in Cappadocia." Having thus bravely proclaimed Christ and refuted the falsely-named error of the idols, she was bound and cast into prison. When her servant Eroteis heard of this, she entered the prison and kissed her mistress's bonds. Thereupon, the holy Capitolina is beheaded; as for the holy Eroteis, she was first cast into the fire and emerged unharmed; then, being coerced to sacrifice to the idols and refusing to be persuaded, she was later pierced with a sword and was perfected.
ON THE SAME MONTH: THE TWENTY-EIGHTH DAY
The contest of the holy hieromartyr Cyriacus, who revealed the precious cross to holy Helena.
After he had revealed the precious and life-giving cross and had been baptized, he became bishop of Jerusalem. He prolonged his life until the times of Julian the Apostate; this emperor, having undertaken an expedition against the Persians, went off to Jerusalem. Having learned about the saint, he sent [men] who apprehended him and brought him to his own tribunal. And attempting to force him to sacrifice to the idols, he was rebuked by him. Whence he ordered his right hand to be amputated, saying, "It is just that the hand which through its letters has turned many away from the worship of the gods should be cut off." Then he commanded molten lead to be poured into his mouth; and presently he directed him to be placed upon a heated bronze bed. When his mother came to him, she too was seized; and being hung by her hair from a height, she was torn asunder until she breathed her last. Cyriacus, for his part, was cast into a cauldron and, pierced by a spear, he was perfected.
On the same day.
Commemoration of holy John the Chozebite.
Born of wealthy parents in Thebes of Egypt, when he came to mature age, he would have no communion with the Catholic Church on account of the synod that was held at Chalcedon. But when, at some point, the desire to visit the holy places had seized him, he seemed to himself in his sleep to be laboring much and employing every effort to enter in and adore the precious cross, yet unable to find the gate. And being greatly distressed, he heard a voice saying to him: "The Cross does not accept those who despise the communion of the Catholic Church." Having awakened, he received communion with the Catholic Church and immediately betook himself to the nearby deserts, and he withdrew to the mountain of Chozeba until his death, where he practiced every kind of virtue and performed miracles. Finally, having trained many in holy instructions and religious exercises and having enticed them to enter upon this same way of life, he passed away and was buried on the same mountain.
MONTH OF OCTOBER, 29TH DAY. Commemoration of our holy Father Abramius.
The holy Abramius was born to Christian parents. Against his will, he was married to a woman; but for the sake of Christ, he left her, withdrew, and shut himself within a small dwelling for many years. Then he was ordained a presbyter. When his brother passed away, leaving behind a female child of about seven years, the saint took her and shut her in another cell near his own. In her twentieth year, through the envy of the devil, it happened that she fell into fornication and went to stay in an inn among harlots. When the saint learned of this, he donned a soldier's uniform, left his cell, and mounting a horse, he drew her back from her fall and took her back to her own cell. She showed such repentance that she even performed miracles, and in this way she completed her life. The holy Abramius fell asleep in a very advanced old age.
On the same day: The contest of the holy martyrs Claudius, Asterius, Neon, and Theonilla.
These lived under the Emperor Diocletian and Lysias, the governor of Cilicia. They were young in age and brothers by law. Having a stepmother, they went to the city of Mopsuestia to recover their patrimony and to pursue the matter before the governor. B The stepmother, not wishing to give them anything, denounced them as Christians. Upon being seized and questioned, they confessed the name of Christ with boldness. Whereupon, being brought into the theater, they were hung up naked, tortured, and scourged with great severity. Then, after being cruelly vexed with many other punishments, they were all bound together in one chain by the soldiers and led outside the walls of the city; there they were suspended from wood and, being fastened with nails, they met their death. After them, the holy Theonilla was brought in; she too was suspended by her hair and, being beaten with rods for many hours, she gave up her spirit to the Lord.
MONTH OF THE SAME, 30TH DAY. The contest of the holy martyr Epimachus.
The martyr Epimachus was a private man, fair in appearance, and from the region of Egypt. Being a Christian, he was deeply pained to see the governor of the region raging against the holy martyrs with tortures in the city of Alexandria. Therefore, on a certain day, when the governor was forcing the saints to sacrifice to idols and the altar was set before them, Epimachus, filled with divine zeal, rushed to overturn the altar. Upon this, he was seized and delivered to prison. But even there, he exhorted the saints to bear witness for Christ with readiness. When the saints were led out of prison and rebuked the governor, the governor asked them whence they had acquired such boldness. And for many hours, she rendered up her spirit to the
Lord. A Having learned that this was caused by Epimachus, he ordered him to be hoisted up and torn to pieces; and when his blood flowed onto the extinguished eye of a certain girl standing below, who, having been blind in one eye, was looking at the saint and lamenting his fate, she immediately received her sight. Disturbed by this, the governor ordered him to be beheaded, and thus he met his end.
31st Day. The Contest of the Holy Martyr Eutropia.
The martyr Eutropia was born in Alexandria of Egypt. When a great multitude of martyrs were imprisoned in the same city, taking pity on them, she would sometimes visit them in person, and at other times provide for their necessary expenses through others. Because of this, she was denounced to the prefect Apellianus, arrested, and cast into prison. Being led out from there, she was placed before his tribunal; having confessed Christ, she was stretched on the rack and scorched with torches. In this torment, she cried out to the torturers in a loud voice, saying, "The water that you pour upon me brings me great refreshment!" For she considered the flames to be like water, and she affirmed that she beheld a terrible man similar to the Son of God, whom even the soldiers and all who were present also saw. Saying these things and giving thanks to Christ, for whom she willingly endured such torments, she surrendered her blessed soul to Him.
The Same Day. The Contest of the Holy Hieromartyr Zenobius and his sister Zenobia.
The martyr Zenobius lived during the reign of the emperor Diocletian; he was the son of Zenodotus and Thecla, who were Christians. He also had a sister named Zenobia. As he grew in age and in the knowledge of God and piety, he was ordained bishop of the city of Aegeae, where he was born. He performed many healings in the name of Christ, such that he cured diseases which were deemed incurable by physicians. Being arrested, he was brought before the prefect Lysias. When his sister Zenobia learned of this, she offered herself voluntarily. They were first scourged and placed upon a red-hot iron bed; then they were cast into a boiling cauldron filled with pitch. Being delivered from such torments by the grace of God, they were again compelled, by the madness of the prefect, to sacrifice to the impure demons. But as they remained in their resolve, they received their end by the sword; and thus they consumed their martyrdom.
The Same Day. Commemoration of the Holy Apostles Stachys, Amplias, Urbanus, and those with them.
The holy apostle Stachys was ordained bishop of Byzantium by the most holy apostle Andrew, who had built a church on the other side at Argyropolis, in which two thousand Christians gathered, whom Stachys taught. He held the episcopate for sixteen years, and he rested.
in peace. Amplias, however, bishop of Ulyssipolis, which is in Macedonia, was killed by the Greeks. Urban, bishop of Macedonia, was likewise killed by Jews and Greeks along with many others. And thus it happened on one and the same day that others in different places, nations, and cities, having been tormented in various ways by those who were ignorant of God—that is, by Greeks and gentiles—rendered their blessed souls to the Lord. Their precious relics were later transferred to Constantinople and were deposited in the place called Pegae.
MONTH OF NOVEMBER
The first day
The Dormition of the holy Unmercenaries Cosmas and Damian, sons of holy Theodota.
Cosmas and Damian, the holy Unmercenaries, were the sons of a certain Christian woman named Theodota; from her they were taught the true faith and the Holy Scriptures, and from the Holy Spirit they were taught the medical art. They applied themselves to healing not only men but also beasts, without receiving any payment from anyone. When they had healed a woman named Palladia, she came secretly to Saint Damian and offered him three eggs. When Saint Cosmas learned of this, he commanded that the relics of Damian should not be placed near his own after death. When Saint Cosmas had died first, and then Damian, as the people were deliberating where Damian should be buried, a camel, which had been afflicted by a demon but had been healed by the saints, suddenly spoke with a human voice, declaring that the relics of Damian ought to be buried in the same tomb as Cosmas, since he had not accepted the eggs as payment, but had been compelled by an oath. And thus both were buried together.
The same day
The contest of the holy women martyrs Cyriaena and Juliana.
Cyriaena the martyr lived under the Emperor Maximian, and was from the city of Tarsus in Cilicia. Having been seized by the prefect of that same city, and being much compelled to deny Christ and sacrifice to idols, she remained most constant in her purpose. For this reason, her head and eyebrows were shaved, and, her forehead having been covered with a libellous placard, she was set upon an ass, beaten with switch-rods by idle boys, and led naked through the city of Tarsus by mimes and unruly soldiers. But when she prayed that she might not be seen naked, she was covered by divine grace and escaped the eyes of the idolaters. After these torments, she was brought to the city of Rhaedestus, along with another Christian woman named Juliana, who was from the same city, and they were cast into the fire; and both, glorifying God in the furnace and giving thanks to Him, rendered up their blessed souls.
On the same day
The contest of the holy martyrs John, bishop, and Jacob, presbyter, surnamed the Zealot.
These men, under Sapor, king of the Persians, were teaching the word of the faith in Persia, and converting many from idols to the Lord. Now on one day, the bishop John saw in his sleep a certain executioner, who held a crown that he carried upon the hilt of a sword, setting it upon his own head and saying to him: "Receive, O bishop, the crown you have so greatly desired." A few days later, he himself and Jacob the presbyter were seized by the infidels, and having been tried first with various tortures, they were then slain by the sword, giving thanks to the Lord our God to the very end that they had been deemed worthy to meet death for Him. And thus it happened to the bishop just as he had seen in his dream, having obtained the crown of incorruptible life; and they were honorably buried in the same place by faithful and pious men.
OF THE SAME MONTH.
The second day.
 The contest of the holy martyrs Acindynus, Pegasius, Aphthonius, Anempodistus, Elpidephorus, and their companions.
Of these saints, Acindynus, Pegasius, and Anempodistus were of the region of Persia during the reign of Constantine the Great. While they were living in their cells, they were teaching the word of God. Wherefore, having been seized by Sapor, king of the Persians, they were subjected to tortures: whom, when they had rendered mute through their prayers, they later restored the faculty of speech; then they were cast into cauldrons filled with boiling lead, but emerged unharmed. Aphthonius, who was a soldier, seeing this miracle, was vehemently moved and believed in Christ, and was immediately led outside the walls and beheaded. But they, sewn into ox-hides, were cast into the sea; and the holy Aphthonius, appearing with angels, brought them out onto the mainland. And because they had escaped ship-wreck, the hands of the soldiers who had cast them headlong into the sea were cut off. Elpidephorus, however, a man of the senatorial order, roused by these miracles, rebuked the king; and seven thousand men believed with him, who were all condemned to beheading. Those who remained were twenty-eight men, who, together with Sapor's mother, who had embraced the faith of Christ, were consumed by fire.
On the same day
The contest of the holy martyrs Atticus, Eudoxius, Agapius, and their companions.
These holy soldiers were in the city of Sebaste in the times of the Emperor Licinius; and they all took counsel together to remain in the faith of Christ with all the soldiers. Wherefore, they were tortured by the dux Marcellus and thrust into prison. Being led out from there, their backs and loins were most cruelly lashed with sinews, and their teeth were pulled out. While they were being tortured, the dux turned to Saint Carterius: "Do you alone," he said, "dare to...
stir up the people to defect from the emperor’s faith?" To which he replied: "I was the author for the people, not that they should depart from the faith of the emperor, but that they should follow the immortal King Christ." After these things, Styracius, Tobilas, and Nicopolitianus were brought; and when they persisted in the confession of Christ, by order of Marcellus they were beaten with clubs, and after being tormented by many other tortures, together with many others who had believed in Christ, they were condemned to the fire and ended their lives.
NOVEMBER.
EODEM MENSE. B 
Die tertia.
163 The contest of the holy martyrs Acepsimas, bishop, Joseph, presbyter, and Aithalas, deacon.
These saints also suffered martyrdom in Persia under Sapor the tyrant, during the times of Constantine the Great, emperor of the Romans. Now Saint Acepsimas was a bishop of venerable gray hair (being in his eightieth year), his body still firm, tall, and upright: furthermore, he was a man of great wealth. Having been arrested with his companions, he was brought before the prince of the magi; and because he could not be persuaded to sacrifice to the Sun and to Mars, but rather heaped insults upon them, he was most severely beaten and cast into chains. Then Joseph was brought, who was from the same region and himself an old man, and because he had contradicted the archimagus, he was beaten with clubs until his flesh was foully lacerated and his bones were laid bare: then he was thrust into prison along with Saint Acepsimas. After these, Aithalas was also brought forth; and with his hands bound to his knees, his limbs were so dislocated that the entire structure of his body was dissolved. Finally, Acepsimas expired amidst the flames; Joseph, suspended head downward, was stoned; and Aithalas, also suspended head downward, gave up his spirit.
Eodem die
164 Commemoration of the holy Father Joannicius the wonderworker.
Our great father Joannicius was from the region of Bithynia during the times of the Emperor Constantine Caballinus. Because of his bravery, he served in the military unit known as the Excubitors, and he acted valiantly in all wars, slaughtering the race of the Bulgarians. But during one battle, having seen a great multitude of his fellow soldiers slaughtered, and being filled with grief, he abandoned the military life. He reached Mount Olympus and was living in quietude. Then, having been recognized by chance by a fellow soldier, he withdrew to the mountain of Lycia, where, after having performed many miracles, he was prompted by divine revelation to go to the monastery of Eristas, and he became a monk. Then, moving from mountain to mountain, he performed wonders everywhere and routed demons. He also received the gift of prophecy, and foretold the successions of emperors and patriarchs. Finally, three days after Saint Methodius had been made patriarch, just as he had predicted, he fell asleep in the Lord.
OF THE SAME MONTH. THE FOURTH DAY.
A 185 The contest of the holy martyr Porphyrius.
Porphyrius the martyr was from the city of Ephesus, during the reign of Aurelian; he was an excellent actor of theatrical plays, and Alexander, the Count of the Caesareans, having been delighted by his playful art, took him along with him to Caesarea. It happened that a feast day of the demons was being celebrated with the greatest pomp of games, in which the rites of the Christians were also represented in jest. Porphyrius was therefore brought forward so that he might be baptized by one who had donned the role of a bishop; water having been brought, he entered it naked, while the false bishop, in the Christian rite, said: "In the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, Porphyrius is baptized." When this was done, angels appeared, who, having clothed Porphyrius in a white stole, chanted with torches, saying: "As many of you as have been baptized into Christ have put on Christ, Alleluia." Moved by this miracle, many believed in the Lord. The Count, truly suffused with shame, departed; and the following day, having brought Porphyrius forward and falsely accused him as a magician, he ordered him to be beheaded.
On the same day.
186 Commemoration of the holy apostles from the number of the seventy: Patrobas, Hermes, Linus, Gaius, and Philologus.
All these were apostles from the number of the seventy disciples. And Patrobas, indeed, of whom the great Apostle Paul makes mention in his Epistles, became bishop of Puteoli, and having converted many to the faith of Christ, he baptized them. Hermes, however, of whom the apostle Paul also makes mention in his Epistle to the Romans, was bishop of Philippopolis. Furthermore, Linus, after the most holy apostle Peter, was named bishop of great Rome. Gaius, indeed, of whom the same apostle makes mention, was proclaimed bishop of the Ephesians after holy Timothy. Finally, Philologus, of whom the same apostle makes mention, was created bishop of Sinope by Andrew, who was called the first of all the apostles. Exercising therefore their episcopate in the mentioned cities, and having endured many troubles and vexations for the word of piety, and having instructed many peoples and led them to Christ, they departed.
OF THE SAME MONTH. THE FIFTH DAY.
187 The contest of the holy martyrs Galaction and Episteme.
Saint Galaction was from the city of Emesa, born according to the prediction of a certain monk, Onuphrius, by whom his parents had also been baptized. But when, after the death of his mother, his father gave him a wife by the name of Episteme, most beautiful but still an unbeliever, the wedding having been celebrated, Galaction indeed approached his wife, but could by no means be brought to admit her to a kiss; and being asked by her the reason for this matter, he said: "Because,"
you are not a Christian." Upon hearing this, she immediately promised that she would become a Christian. Since there was no priest available on account of the persecution, she was baptized by Galaction himself. Then, by agreement between them, they distributed their wealth to the poor and withdrew to a mountain, and took refuge separately in monasteries. When a persecution against the Christians arose at that time, and the ruler of the region learned of them, he sent and seized Galaction. And as he was being dragged away, Episteme caught up with him, and was seized together with him by the ruler; after having their hands and feet cut off, they were beheaded.
On the same day.
The Contest of Saint Domninus, and his companions Theotimus, Timotheus, Philotheus, Dorotheus, Carterius, and Silvanus.
These flourished under the Emperor Maximinus. As they were teaching the word of God and converting many of the unbelievers to the Lord, they were denounced to Maximinus; and being seized, they were most severely tortured. Then they were sentenced to suffer in the copper mines. After a long time, having been brought out of the prison and set before the iniquitous tribunal, and since they could not be persuaded to sacrifice to idols, they were placed on the rack and tormented most cruelly. Observing their steadfastness, a certain man named Pamphilus, considering within himself that they could not be equal to such punishments unless they were supported by a firm hope in the greatest things, believed in Christ, along with three virgin women. Wherefore, covered with shame, Maximinus pronounced that they should all be put to death. And all of them, being beheaded, delivered their holy and blessed souls to the Lord.
ON THE SAME MONTH.
The sixth day.
The Contest of Saint Paul the Confessor, Patriarch of Constantinople.
This man was originally from Thessalonica, a notary to the most holy Patriarch Alexander, and a deacon of the Church in Constantinople; whom the orthodox, after the death of Alexander, elected as patriarch. But the Emperor Constantius, being an Arian, having returned from Antioch, expelled him from the throne, and in his place appointed Eusebius of Nicomedia. Paul reaches Rome, and finds there the great Athanasius also cast out from his own throne; and through letters from the Emperor Constans, both were restored to their respective thrones. But through the machinations of the Arians they were again expelled by Constantius. Then Constans wrote to his brother Constantius that if they were not restored to their own thrones, he would come against him with weapons of war. Therefore, Paul received his throne for a third time. After the death of Constans, he was exiled to Cucusus in Armenia. And having been shut up in a chamber, while he was celebrating the liturgy, he was strangled by the Arians with his own omophorion.
Eodem die
A 170 Commemoration of the dust, or ash, or stacte (frankincense-type resin) that fell from the sky.
In the final days of the great Emperor Leo, surnamed the Bess, at the sixth hour of the day, the sky was covered with black clouds, such as is wont to happen when great downpours are imminent; presenting a burning appearance in every direction, it seemed as if it were about to consume all things with fire. And although from clouds of this kind rain is usually brought down, it seemed that, no differently than Sodom of old, it would incinerate everything with fire and flame. But when all the priests and pious men fled with tears to the temples, and suppliantly besought God and the immaculate Mother of God, the most clement Lord, who desires not the death of sinners, tempering punishment with clemency, commanded the clouds to send forth an extraordinary shower. It rained, therefore, from evening until midnight. But that which flowed down was boiling stacte, like coals, which scorched all plants; and that which fell upon the tiles rose to the span of a man’s hand.
IN THE SAME MONTH. The seventh day.
171 The contest of the holy martyrs Antonius and his parents, Melasippus and Casina.
These were in the city of Ancyra during the tyranny of Julian the Apostate. They were apprehended by him. And while Antonius was thrown into prison, his parents were suspended on the rack, torn asunder, and burned with fire; and Casina’s breasts were cut off, and Melasippus’s legs were severed. But when Antonius was brought forth and saw his parents suspended, he kissed their members lying upon the ground and anointed himself with the blood dripping from them. They, indeed, remaining suspended, migrated to the Lord. Then Antonius, who was in his thirteenth year, having spat in the face of the Apostate, was himself suspended and torn, his ankles were bored, and his head was shaved. Then, being sent to the dux Agrippinus and thrown by him into a furnace, when he emerged unharmed, he drew forty boys, moved by the magnitude of the event, to the faith of Christ: all of whom, together with him, received their desired end, killed by the sword.
Eodem die
172 The contest of Saint Hieron and his two and thirty companions, who suffered martyrdom in the city of Melitine.
The holy martyrs Hieron, Nicander, Hesychius, and thirty other companions flourished in the city of Melitine in Armenia under the impious emperors Maximian and Diocletian. When a general persecution was raging against all Christians through slaughter, they were detained by the prefect of Melitine and tried with many tortures, to see if they could be led from the worship of Christ to the superstition of idols. But when not only did they refuse to do this, but even declared with a loud voice that Christ is the true God, and the maker of heaven and earth and of all things that are therein,
they were first stripped and beaten until all their entrails were exposed. Afterwards they were cast into prison, utterly deprived of all care, and finally, after long torments and being brought out of custody, when they were again urged to sacrifice to idols, and could not be swayed to do so, they were put to the sword.
Eodem die
The Contest of the Holy Martyrs Auctus, Taurion, and Thessalonica
The martyr Thessalonica was the daughter of a certain Cleon, a priest of idols. Having become a Christian, she was compelled by her father to deny Christ. Because she refused to do this, she was stripped naked and beaten most severely with scourges, and her sides were crushed with clubs. Then, being disinherited of her father's goods, she dwelt on a small piece of property which had fallen to her from her mother’s inheritance. When two pious Christians, named Auctus and Taurion, learned of what was happening, they were greatly indignant at Cleon. For this reason, he denounced them to the prefect of the region, and they were seized and flogged; then they were pelted with stones and cast into a fire. Soon after being cast into a lake, when they had escaped even from that unharmed, they were beheaded. Thessalonica, moreover, having never ceased to confess Christ thereafter, died in peace; and her precious relics were deposited in Amphipolis with the other saints honorably and magnificently.
EODEM MENSE.
Die octava
The Synaxis of the Archangels
When the all-good and merciful God willed to create this world, He first conceived of the heavenly powers and the holy angels, and the thought became an act. One of the leaders of their ranks, who at that time was called Sabael, but after his fall was named the Devil, was seized with arrogance; and, thinking himself to be God, he fell from his dignity and was cast out of the heavens along with the order over which he presided. But the archangel Michael, who led another order of angels, when he saw the fall of the deserter, gathered together the choirs of angels. And saying, "Let us attend," he praised the God of all in approximately these words: "Let us attend, we who have been brought forth from nothing and stand before the great and only God, to what has befallen those who were until now light with us, but have now become darkness." Such a gathering of the angels was therefore called a *synaxis*, that is, attention, concord, and union.
Eodem die
The Commemoration of Saint Matrona
She lived during the reign of Leo the Great and was from Perge in Pamphylia. Having been joined to a husband and having borne one daughter, she came to Constantinople with her husband, being fifteen years of age. And having become acquainted with a virgin
named Eugenia, and having zealously embraced her way of life, she did not depart from the churches of God. A Having an even more ardent love for God, and having heard the voice of the divine Gospel saying: "Whosoever desires to come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me," she entrusted her daughter to a certain pious woman to be reared; and having left her husband and donned male attire, she entered the monastery of the holy Bassianus and embraced the monastic life. But when it was known that she was a woman, she was sent by the holy man to Jerusalem; from where, after a few years, she returned to Constantinople, and living a solitary life, she passed away.
OF THE SAME MONTH. THE NINTH.
B 176 The Contest of the holy martyr Alexander of Thessalonica.
Alexander the martyr was under the emperor Maximian; and having been seized, he was compelled to sacrifice to the idols. But he, not being persuaded, but rather moved by divine zeal, and seeing the table of sacrifices standing there, rushed up and, kicking it with his foot, overturned it and poured out the sacrifices. Wherefore, Maximianus, being greatly moved, pronounced that he be punished with the ultimate penalty. When, therefore, the executioner was seeking the neck of the holy man with his sword drawn, he was struck with a sudden stupor. To whom the emperor said: "Why do you stand, and why do you not execute the command?" But he replied: "Seeing a vision, I am struck with great fear." But when the saint had asked for an hour for prayer and had obtained it, then finally, after he had prayed, he was struck by the executioner. And Maximianus, having perceived his soul being borne on high by four angels, permitted his relics to be taken for burial in the city of Thessalonica.
ON THE SAME DAY.
177 The Contest of the holy martyr Antoninus. 
Antoninus the martyr was from the region of the Syrians, exercising the trade of a stone-cutter. But when he saw the Greeks going into the temple of the idols, and sacrificing to the idols, he exhorted them to depart from that superstition. But when by speaking he accomplished nothing, he took it badly and made for the wilderness; and having encountered a certain servant of God, an anchorite by the name of Theotimus, he remained with him for two years. Having afterwards received his blessing, he returned to his homeland to the people seduced by the sorceries of demons, and found them celebrating a feast day for the demons; and having entered their temple, he broke all the idols. Wherefore, having been seized, he was most severely beaten. Then, having set out for Apamea of Syria, when he had obtained from the most holy bishop that he might build a temple in the name of the holy Trinity, and the foundations had already been laid, the townspeople attacked the man by night, cut him limb from limb: and in this manner he rendered up his spirit to God.
ON THE SAME DAY.
178 The Contest of the holy martyr Orestes.
The martyr Orestes was from the city of Tyana,
in Cappadocia, and, despising the idols, he preached Christ openly. He was seized by Maximus, who had recently been appointed governor of the province by Diocletian.
