Wherefore, having observed the time when he was officiating the sacred rites, they rushed into the church. After putting to flight with swords and clubs those who were around him, they cruelly murdered the holy man upon the very altar, and he yielded his spirit to the Lord.
On the same day. Contest of the holy Hieromartyr Cornutus
The holy martyr Cornutus was from the city of Iconium, a pious presbyter who feared God. When a persecution arose against the Christians, the governor of Iconium, a cruel and inhuman man, was sent to torture the Christians living there. Since all others had fled out of fear, Cornutus, abandoned by all, went to meet the governor. Being questioned by him, he boldly confessed Christ and proclaimed himself a perfect Christian; he exposed the deception of the idols and mocked the governor himself. For this reason he was seized, and having had his feet bound with thin cords, he was dragged along the streets and lanes of the city of Iconium until his blood
flowed like a river upon the ground. And being laid out as if dead, he gave thanks to God, offering prayers. And so, having been tortured for a long time, and calling upon Christ until his very last breath, at the prefect’s command he was beheaded, and committed his soul to God.
Eodem die
The Martyrdom of the Holy Martyr Theodore of Alexandria
This holy martyr Theodore, being a pious and virtuous Christian, publicly preached Christ and confessed Him to be God and the Creator of all things. The people of Alexandria, addicted to the superstition of idols, gathered together, seized him, and tortured him severely. Then, plaiting a crown of thorns, they placed it upon his head; and beating him with rods, they led him bound through the city, exposing him to mockery and jeering. After this, they threw him into the sea. But a gentle breeze brought him safely to land. And having been seized again, he was brought bound before the prefect. When he could not turn him from the faith of Christ, he subjected him to torments again; while he, giving glory to God in the very midst of the torments, gave thanks to His holy name. Finally, condemned by the sentence of the prefect to be beheaded, he was led by the soldiers to the place of execution and beheaded. And thus, having completed his martyrdom, he delivered up his spirit.
OF THE SAME MONTH.
The Martyrdom of the Holy Martyrs Macrobius, Gordianus, and their Companions
These holy martyrs flourished during the reign of Licinius. Macrobius was from Cappadocia, and Gordianus from Paphlagonia. Being young in age, and endowed with bravery and prudence, they were appointed to serve at the king’s table, and because of their virtue and the beauty of their faces, they were greatly loved by him. But having been identified as Christians, and being compelled by entreaties at times and by threats at others to deny Christ, and refusing to be persuaded, they were exiled to the region of Scythia. Having arrived there, they found the holy Zoticus, Lucianus, and Heli striving and contending for the confession of Christ. And after many torments, Zoticus and Lucianus were finished by the sword. But they, together with Heli, were thrown into prison. And he was beheaded, while Macrobius and Gordianus were finished by fire. Valerianus himself breathed his last while weeping at the tomb of the saints.
Eodem die
The Martyrdom of the Holy Hieromartyr Julian of Ancyra in Galatia
During the reign of Licinius, there was a great persecution against the Christians. Now there was a pious priest near Ancyra of Galatia by the name of Julian. On account of the persecution, he fled and went off into
a mountain. And he was being hidden in a cave, together with forty-two other holy men. But when the governor of Ancyra came near to the mountain for the sake of sacrificing at a temple of one of the gods of the Greeks, called Hecate, and also to the temple of the governor, they were sought after, so that the holy men might be captured. But as the holy men went out day by day, one by one, to draw water from a nearby spring, and others were receiving it, it fell to holy Julian to fill it with water. And some of the Greeks, seeing him, seized him, and having bound him, they brought him before the governor. But when the governor questioned him concerning the others, he did not betray any of them, but freely professed Christ. A helmet fastened to his head is placed upon him; then having been scraped and severely tortured, he was beheaded.
EODEM MENSE.
Die decima quarta
37 Commemoratio Exaltationis pretiosæ et vivificæ Crucis.
Constantine the Great, the first of the Christian emperors, was once waging war, as some say, in Rome against Maxentius, before he gained the empire; but as others believe, against the Scythians by the river Danube. But when he saw numerous hostile forces, he was sick at heart and at a loss for counsel, doubting the outcome of the affair. But at night, seeing the precious cross in the heavens as if inscribed by the stars: "Constantine, by this conquer," he fashioned a cross very similar to it for himself, and conquered the enemies. Wherefore, having been baptized by his mother Helena, he wished to find the precious cross on which the Lord was crucified. And his mother went to Jerusalem, and found it revealed by Judas Cyriacus, who later became bishop: and when all the people had seen it, they were affected with the greatest joy. And the empress, indeed, with the senate, adored it and kissed it. But the people, when they were seized with the greatest desire to adore the cross, and could not because of the numberless crowd of men, asked that they might at least see it, and the, people began to cry out, "Lord, have mercy," and the exaltation was formed. When he saw this, the people erupted into this voice. Kyrie eleison. And hence the type of the Exaltation was expressed.
Eodem die D 
38 Certamen sancti martyris Papæ.
Papas, a martyr of Christ of many contests, was from the region of Lycia, in the time of Emperor Maximian. When he was teaching the right faith, and freely professing Christ, and persuading many of the Greeks to believe in Him, he was denounced to the governor, and having been seized by him, he was first beaten very severely, and all the bones of his face were broken. Then, having been suspended, he was scraped for a long time. And after these things, iron shoes having long and sharp nails were put upon him. And he was driven forward, being beaten in front of the horses, from his own city to the city of Diocaesarea, and from there to Seleucia. And as he was running, a certain woman, having met him, wiped his blood with a linen cloth, and kissed
it as she wept. Then, being placed under a wild fig tree, and beaten severely, he gave up his spirit to the Lord. And after his death, the fig tree became fruitful.
ON THE SAME MONTH. THE FIFTEENTH DAY.
The Martyrdom of the holy great martyr Nicetas.
The glorious martyr of Christ, Nicetas, lived during the reign of Constantine the Great; he was a Goth by birth, noble and wealthy among his own people, and was instructed in the faith by Theophilus, the most religious bishop of Gothia. When the whole Gothic nation had been divided into two factions, and each had created its own prince, one of the princes fled to the emperor of the Romans. The emperor, having launched an expedition against the other Gothic ruler, defeated him and subdued the Goths; and from that time the race of Christians multiplied among them, with Saint Nicetas being the first, and preaching the faith in Christ to the people with boldness. But after the emperor had returned, the infidel Goths, incited by fury, stirred up a persecution against the Christians; and they tortured others in various ways, but Saint Nicetas, after being tormented with many tortures, was handed over to the fire. C
On the same day. Commemoration of our holy Father Philotheus the Wonderworker.
Philotheus the Wonderworker was from the theme of Opsikion; he was a priest, and a great worshiper of God from his youth; for he was assiduously devoted to fasting and prayers; he distributed his wealth to the poor; and he spent his days in mourning and weeping, because he contemplated the punishments of sinners in the next world, and the hell of fire, and the sleepless worm. As he therefore strengthened many, and helped them through his teaching, and served God blamelessly, he received the gift of working wonders, to cast out demons, to heal the sick, to cleanse the lepers, and to bring down rain during the greatest droughts; for the Lord does the will of those who fear Him, and hears their supplications. Thus working wonders, and readily granting the requests to those who approached him, he ended his life in peace. And having been entombed, there flows miraculously from his precious bones, until today, a healing oil.
On the same day. The Martyrdom of the holy martyrs Maximus, Theodotus, and Asclepiodote.
These were during the reign of Maximian, the persecutor of the Christians, and they were from the city of Marcianopolis in Thrace. While they were teaching the word of God, and walking in all His commandments by night and day, and converting many from the error of idols to our Lord Jesus Christ, they were seized by the general of Thrace. And first, they were beaten with rods, and then flayed, until their bones were exposed. Thereafter, having been beheaded...
of their hands, feet, and ears, they were cast upon the ground as dead men. Being placed upon wagons, they were led to Adrianople, where they were again urged by the general to sacrifice to the idols, at least in word. But since they could not be persuaded to do this, and instead mocked the idols, asserting that the gods of the gentiles are demons and are themselves accursed, along with those who worship them, they were sentenced to death by beheading. And with them, the martyr Asclepiodota, their kinswoman, also reached her end.
On the same day. Commemoration of the most holy Martin, Pope of Rome.
Our holy Father and confessor Martin was Pope of ancient Rome during the reign of Constans, the grandson of Heraclius. Since this Constans was a Monothelite heretic, and Paul, the Patriarch of Constantinople, followed the same heresy, Pope Martin, upon learning of this, admonished the Emperor and the Patriarch by letter to abjure the heresy of the Monothelites. But when he accomplished nothing, he convened a synod at Rome and anathematized such heresy and those addicted to it. When the Emperor learned of this, he summoned Martin to Constantinople, promising that he would do all that he asked, though he was acting through simulation and fraud. Thus, the saint, arriving, was received with the greatest ignominy by the Emperor and the Patriarch, and, having been despoiled of his goods, was cast into a dark prison. Thereafter, he was exiled to the city of Cherson, where, after suffering many grievous things, he died.
On the same day. The martyrdom of the holy martyr Porphyrius.
The martyr Porphyrius lived during the reign of Julian the Apostate; he used to entertain him as he reclined at table with jests and witticisms, practicing the mimic art. But when he saw that he had renounced our Lord Jesus Christ and was worshipping idols, holding them as gods, moved by divine zeal, he rebuked him before the entire senate, calling him a transgressor, impious, lawless, and ungrateful for the benefits and the empire which God had bestowed upon him. Wherefore, Julian, becoming enraged, first tortured him cruelly with various bitter torments; then he lacerated his entire body by beating it with scourges until his bones were laid bare. Finally, he ordered the executioners to behead him. Having received this sentence, he was led bound to the place of execution and, struck by the sword, he surrendered his life, having attained the immortal King in place of a temporal one, and eternal happiness instead of corruptible pleasures.
On the same day. The martyrdom of the holy martyr Melitina.
The martyr Melitina was from the city of Marcianopolis in Thrace, during the reign of the Roman Caesar Antoninus. While she was preaching Christ, she was seized by Antiochus, the governor of that city. And having confessed herself a Christian...
of the same city’s governor, and having confessed herself a Christian, she was beaten severely. Then she was forced to enter the temple of idols and to sacrifice. But having entered, she prayed to God, and the idols fell and were shattered. Because of this, many Greeks believed in Christ, and even the wife of the governor herself. And being beaten again, she was cast into prison. And again, having been brought out of prison, she was hung up naked and her sides were torn. And again, being brought into another idol temple to sacrifice, she prayed; and the idols falling, they were scattered into small pieces. Then having received the sentence by the sword, she was beheaded. Her remains were taken away by a pious man who feared God, Acacius by name, and having carried them away, he laid them to rest on the island of Lemnos.
ON THE SAME MONTH. THE SIXTEENTH.
The contest of the three holy virgins and sisters, Faith, Hope, and Charity, and of their mother Sophia.
The martyr of Christ, Sophia, with her three daughters, was from the region of Italy. And desiring to become a Christian, having taken along her three daughters under the reign of Hadrian, she went to Rome and was baptized with them in secret by the bishop of Rome. And after being baptized, they taught others the things concerning Christ. And they turned many to the Lord. But when these things were known, they were seized by Hadrian and tortured. And first, the holy Faith, being twelve years of age, having been hung up and torn, she was beheaded. Then her second sister, Hope, being ten years of age, having been beaten with rods, she too was sentenced to beheading. And the third, the holy Charity, being in her ninth year, having been cast into a furnace and having come out unharmed, she was cut down by the sword. Their mother, Sophia, giving thanks to God for all things, buried them, and having fallen down upon the grave and wept, she gave up her soul.
On the same day.
The contest of the holy martyr Agathoclia.
The martyr Agathoclia was by profession a Christian, but a slave of a certain Nicolas and Paulina his wife, who had previously been Christians, but then departed from Christ and worshipped idols. Therefore, the martyr was also urged to deny Christ and to come over to the idols. But as she could not be persuaded to do this, she was tormented for eight years, being examined daily with various tortures. Sometimes she was beaten with rough stones on the spine and the face; at other times with rods on the head and shoulders. As she could not be persuaded to deny Christ, she was crushed with an iron hammer; then, her tongue was cut out, and she was locked in prison, with an order having been given that no one should bring her food. But by the providence of God, a bird called a nightingale, bringing small
fruits, fed her. Afterward her mistress entering the prison, destroyed her with a red-hot iron bar.
A 
EODEM MENSE. Die decima septima.
Contest of the holy martyrs Lucia the widow, and Geminianus her spiritual son.
The martyr Lucia shone in the city of Rome during the reign of Maximian, being highly wealthy and illustrious. When she was seventy-five years of age, and had completed her thirty-sixth year in widowhood, she had a legitimate son named Eutropius, who was addicted to the superstition of idols. She was unable to persuade him to come to Christ, but rather was denounced by him to Maximian. Having been seized, and refusing to sacrifice to the idols, she was cast into a cauldron filled with boiling lead and pitch. But having emerged from it unharmed and unscathed, she was led through the city and paraded about, and beaten. Saint Geminianus met her, followed her, and shortly after being baptized, was called her spiritual son. Together with her, he came into the sight of the emperor and confessed Christ. For this reason, he was tortured. But being led by a holy angel to Tauromenium, there Geminianus was executed by the sword by the governor, and Lucia fell asleep in peace.
B 
EODEM MENSE. Die decima octava.
Contest of the holy hieromartyr Simeon, bishop of Jerusalem.
The holy martyr Simeon flourished under the Emperor Trajan, being the second bishop of Jerusalem after James, the brother of the Lord. He was a cousin of the Lord himself and of James, being the son of Cleophas. For this man and Joseph, who was called the father of the Lord according to the flesh, or rather named so, were lawful brothers. This Simeon, being accused by the Jews of preaching Christ, was seized by the prefects of the Greeks and beaten severely. Having been examined with many tortures, he was shut up in prison, to be consumed by hunger and filth. Being brought out from there, since he could in no way be induced to deny Christ, he was nailed to a cross and met his death, being one hundred and twenty years of age. His sacred body was committed to the earth by his relatives, but his blessed soul, ascending into heaven, rejoices eternally with the saints in Christ Jesus our Lord.
Eodem die.
Commemoration of holy Eumenius, bishop of Gortyna, the wonderworker.
Eumenius the wonderworker was from his youth a pious Christian, fearing God. Although he was rich, he distributed all his possessions to the poor. He himself spent all the days of his life in fastings and prayers; and he diligently followed that exhortation of the great apostle
For he rejoiced with those who rejoiced and wept with those who wept. He always walked about with eyes suffused with tears. He also made it his practice never to entertain slander against anyone, nor to condemn a man. But if ever he heard of some transgression against a person, he thought that he himself was the one who had committed it. Therefore, for such virtues of his, being chosen by the clergy and the people, he was ordained bishop of Gortyna, on the island of Crete. And having lived in the episcopate for many years, and having taught many people the things that pertain to the kingdom of heaven, and having received the grace of many miracles, he fell asleep, remaining wondrous even after death up to this day.
On the same day.
The Contest of the holy martyr Ariadne.
The martyr Ariadne lived under the emperors Hadrian and Antoninus. She was a Christian, but a slave of a certain wealthy man who held a position of authority in the region of Phrygia. Now on one of the days when the birthday of her master’s son was being celebrated, she was compelled to enter the temple of the idols along with them, and to keep the feast. Since she would not be persuaded, she was beaten mercilessly and thrown into prison, and she was starved severely. Being taken out of prison, she was delivered to the governor of the region to be punished. And being again compelled by him to sacrifice to the idols, and not being persuaded, she was suspended and her sides were scraped. Released from prison and finding an opportunity, she fled toward the mountain. When the soldiers learned of this, they pursued her with swords. And seeing that she was about to be massacred, she prayed to God to be delivered from them. And the rock having been split by the command of God, she entered into it; the rock having closed again and been joined together, it became a tomb for her as she gave thanks and departed.
ON THE SAME MONTH.
19th.
The Contest of the holy martyrs Trophimus, Dorymedon, and Sabbatius.
These martyrs lived during the reign of Probus. As the hippodrome games were being held at Antioch in Pisidia, Trophimus and Sabbatius entered the theater, and seeing the Greeks sacrificing to the idols, they groaned from the soul and said to God: "Lord, grant these wandering people to abandon the idols and to acknowledge thee the only God, and thy only-begotten Son, and the Holy Spirit." Having said this, and being recognized as Christians, they were seized by the governor. And first, Trophimus, having been beaten and scraped, was thrown into prison. Then Sabbatius was brought in, and having confessed Christ, he was tortured and torn apart and gave up his spirit. After these things, Trophimus, being taken out of prison and shod in iron shoes, was sent to the governor of the city of Synnada. And having been tortured by him, he was locked up. But Dorymedon came to Trophimus and became a Christian. And being recognized, they were both beheaded.
The same day: The contest of the holy hieromartyr Januarius, bishop of Benevento, and his company.
The holy martyr Januarius flourished during the reign of the Emperor Diocletian and was bishop of the city of Benevento. Because of his confession of Christ, he was cast into chains in Puteoli, along with Sossius, Proculus, Eutychius, and Acustius, the deacons. Timotheus, the prefect of the region, having come to Puteoli and found the saint in bonds, brought him out of prison and cast him into a furnace. But seeing him come out of the furnace unscathed and unhurt, he was seized with greater fury and ordered the executioners to cut the sinews of his feet. The saint having borne this form of punishment bravely, he was again cast into custody. When the clerics Festus and Desiderius came from the city of Benevento for the purpose of visiting him, they too were seized and bound in the prison together with the bishop and the deacons. They were then brought out and condemned to the beasts; being uninjured by them, by the order of the prefect, they were beheaded.
The same day: The contest of the holy one hundred and fifty martyrs, Nilus, Peleus, Elias, and their company.
Among these holy martyrs, one hundred were from Egypt; being Christians and preaching Christ freely, they were seized by the idolaters. Their right eyes were gouged out, and the joints and sinews of their left feet were cut through with a sword. Then, being cast into a furnace, they reached their end. The other fifty were natives of Palestine, and because of Christ they suffered the same punishments as their companions at the hands of the provincial prefect; they were shut up in prison and condemned to work in the mines with the other convicts. Later, they too were brought before the duke, and being urged sometimes by entreaties and sometimes by threats to deny Christ and sacrifice to the idols—and not being persuaded—they received the sentence to be consigned to the flames. A great furnace having therefore been lit, they entered it rejoicing and glorifying God, and thus reached their end.
In the same month: The twentieth day. The commemoration of the holy martyr of Christ, Susanna.
Susanna, the holy martyr, was from the region of Palestine, the daughter of an idol priest named Artemius. After the death of her parents, having been taught the word of truth by the presbyter Silvanus, she was baptized. Then, having distributed her wealth to the poor and abandoned the world, she went to a monastery and embraced the monastic life, wearing male attire and calling herself John, so as not to be recognized by anyone. While she was considered a man, she was accused by a woman moved by the suggestion of the devil, that she had performed an act
of a man upon her; and having been examined by a virgin, and found to be by nature a woman, she put her accuser to shame. Afterwards, having gone to Eleutheropolis, on account of her virtue she was ordained a deaconess by the bishop. And because she proclaimed Christ with boldness, she was seized by the governor. And having been tortured much, and cast into prison, she departed to the Lord.
On the same day, the Contest of the holy great martyr Eustathius, and of his wife Theopistis, and of their children Theopistus and Agapius.
The great martyr Eustathius was a general of the army in Rome, under the Emperor Trajan, himself being called Placidus, and his wife Tatiana. But because of his almsgiving, God wished to provide for his salvation. For as Eustathius was hunting, Christ appeared to him in the shape of a stag, having between its horns a cross shining brighter than the sun, and in the middle the image of Christ; and a voice came forth from the stag saying: "Placidus, why do you persecute me? I am Jesus Christ." Having therefore believed in Him, and been baptized together with his wife, and children, and his whole house, he was so much attacked by the devil, that he not only suffered the loss of his wealth, but also saw his wife led away captive, and his children snatched away by wild beasts, and saw himself naked in a strange land, forced to provide his living by mercenary labor. But God restored to him his wife, and children, and riches. For having been sought after by the emperor, he was placed in his former rank of dignity. But later, having been known to be a Christian, he was cast into a heated brazen bull, together with his wife and children, and was perfected.
ON THE SAME MONTH. THE TWENTY-FIRST.
Commemoration of our holy Fathers Isacius and Meletius, bishops of Cyprus.
The holy and blessed Fathers Isacius and Meletius were bishops of the Church of Cyprus at different times. Isacius indeed earlier, but Meletius later; yet each shone with equal piety. For carrying the fear of God always with them, and being conspicuous in the pontificate, they blamelessly offered the liturgy to God, and readily distributed all their possessions to the poor, and according to the prophet, showing mercy and lending all day long, not only bread, but also the faithful word in teaching. They took care of widows, followed orphans with mercy, raised up dejected spirits, becoming everything to all people, so that they might gain everyone. Having lived thus, they were deemed worthy also of the gift of miracles. And having helped many through their healings, in extreme old age, at different times, as it is said, but nevertheless on the same day of the year, they departed to the God whom they had served with the greatest zeal from their earliest youth.
On the same day: The Contest of the holy martyr Theodore, who suffered martyrdom in Perge of Pamphylia.
The martyr Theodore flourished under the Roman Emperor Antoninus, seventy-six years before the holy Theodore who suffered martyrdom at Amasea. He also served in the legion of recruits, for he was flourishing in his youth, great in stature, and beautiful in appearance. But when he was compelled to sacrifice to the idols, he neglected his earthly military service and the emperor who was prone to corruption, saying, "I have served the heavenly King Christ since I was baptized, and I am unwilling to serve a king on earth." Wherefore, having been seized by the governor and beaten, he was cast into a furnace; yet he came out of it unharmed, not having suffered even the slightest injury. Two soldiers, Socrates and Dionysius, seeing him unharmed by the fire, were astonished by the greatness of the miracle and converted to the faith of Christ; and they were immediately beheaded along with the mother of the holy one, named Philippa, who had come a little while before out of a desire to visit her son. Theodore himself, having first been tortured with various kinds of torments and lastly affixed to a cross, joyfully flew to the heavens.
On the same day: The Contest of the holy hieromartyr and apostle Quadratus, bishop of Magnesia, and his company.
Quadratus, the divine hieromartyr and apostle of Christ, was bishop of the city of Magnesia under the emperors Decius and Valerian. When he heard that Decius was staying in the city of Caesarea, he set out from Magnesia to Nicomedia, in order to visit the holy ones who were shut up in the prisons and to exhort them to endure martyrdom for Christ with a ready spirit; but he was apprehended by the proconsul of Nicomedia. When he was brought to his tribunal along with the other martyrs, he was the first to refute the deception of the idols; and while the others were beaten and again locked up, he himself was sent away to Caesarea to Decius. And when he could not be induced by any artifice to deny Christ, and, being unwilling to yield, was examined by diverse torments and, by the grace of God, delivered from all of them, he was finally cast into a fiercely burning furnace. But even from this he emerged unharmed, and by order of the emperor, he had his holy head cut off and thus reached his end.
On the same day: The Contest of the holy martyr Priscus.
The martyr of Christ, Priscus, burning with love for God and deeply desiring divine baptism, abandoned his ancestral impiety and became a Christian. Wherefore, he wandered far and wide, boldly preaching Christ, inflaming the spirits of all Christians to undergo martyrdom, and also instructing many of the Greeks, converting them to Christ and baptizing them. As he was doing these things, he was denounced by the idolaters to the governor of the region because he was bringing insult upon the greatest gods.
and disregarding the imperial decrees, and persuading everyone to worship and adore a crucified God. Hearing this, the governor was seized by violent wrath and cried out to the soldiers: "Let the evildoer Priscus be brought immediately and be punished." Arrested, he was beaten severely; then, hanging suspended, his sides were raked, and he was pierced with daggers. Finally, being beheaded, he departed to God.
IN THE SAME MONTH.
The twenty-second day.
Contest of the holy hieromartyr Phocas, bishop of Sinope
The holy martyr of Christ, Phocas, was born and raised in the city of Sinope during the reign of Trajan. While he was still a child, he received from God the gift of miracles: for he cast out demons, raised the dead, and saved ships that were in great peril. Having gone from Sinope to the city of Amasea, he performed similar wonders everywhere, and confirmed many in the faith of Christ. After this, returning to Sinope, he was appointed its bishop, and through his miracles he converted many of the Greeks to Christ and baptized them. Later, he learned of his departure through a dove. For it seemed to him in his sleep that he saw a dove come and settle upon his head, offer him a crown, and say: "Receive, Phocas, the symbol of your end." A little while later, Emperor Trajan sent lictors who arrested him. After being tortured severely, he was at last beheaded. Then he was also cast into the fire.
On the same day.
Commemoration of the holy and glorious prophet Jonah
Jonah, the great and glorious prophet, was from the region of the Jews. Being commanded by God to go to the city of Nineveh and proclaim that within three days Nineveh would be overthrown, he, knowing that God is merciful and would grant pardon to the Ninevites if they repented, disobeyed God and, boarding a ship, attempted to flee. But the sea, having become savage at the command of God, sought to devour him. The sailors, casting lots and realizing that they were in danger because of him, took him and threw him into the sea. The sea grew calm, but a sea monster swallowed him, and after three days it cast him onto dry land unharmed. Then, having gone to Nineveh, he carried out the command of God. Afterwards, returning to his own land and taking his mother, he went away into a foreign country; for he was ashamed to dwell in his own land, because his prophecy had been proven false. For God had shown mercy to the Ninevites who repented. Having prophesied many other things as well, he passed away.
On the Same Day [the 22nd]
Commemoration of the holy Jonah, father of Theodore and Theophanis the Confessors.
This Jonah was a presbyter, distinguished for piety and understanding, the father of the most blessed confessor Theodore, and of Theophanis, the writer of the canons. It was this Theodore whose face Leo Caballinus, the iconoclast, marked with ink and wounded, because he could not persuade him to deny the worship and reverence of the holy icons of Christ, of the Theotokos who gave birth to Him without seed, and of all His saints. Having therefore renounced the affairs of the world, the aforementioned holy Jonah retired to the lavra of Saint Sabas and became a monk. He applied himself to such ascesis—fasting, prayer, constant tears, and weeping—that he drew all into admiration of him and inspired many with the desire to imitate his exact and virtuous life. For this reason, he received the gift of healings, by which he benefited those who approached him. Having lived in this manner and having appeared admirable to all, he departed to God.
OF THE SAME MONTH, THE 23RD DAY
Commemoration of the conception of Saint Elizabeth, when she conceived Saint John the Baptist.
Zacharias, the prophet and father of John the Baptist, was a priest of God. Having entered the temple at one time to offer incense, while the Jews stood outside the temple, he saw the angel of God, Gabriel, standing at the right side of the altar of incense, and he was terrified. But the angel, seeing him in distress, said to him: "Do not fear, Zacharias, because your prayer has been heard. And your wife Elizabeth shall bear you a son, and you shall call his name John. And there shall be joy and exultation for you, and many shall rejoice at his birth." But Zacharias, seeing that he himself was elderly and his wife was advanced in age, said: "Lord, how shall this be possible for me?" To whom the angel replied: "I am Gabriel, who stands before God, and I was sent to announce these things to you; and behold, you shall be silent and unable to speak until the day the son is born to you." And he remained deaf and mute until D John was born.
On the Same Day
The Passion of the holy martyr Irais.
Irais, the martyr of Christ, an Alexandrian, a virgin and a nun, having gone out at one time from the monastery to fetch water from the spring for her superior, saw on a ship passing along the seashore the prefect of the city, who was taking with him a multitude of Christian prisoners—presbyters, deacons, holy women, and virgins. And running up, and having inquired of the virgins for what reason they were in chains, and they replied to her...
For the sake of Christ, that we might bear witness for Him and be saved." Hearing these things, Irais entreated the executioners, and they bound her also with the remaining saints. And when the saints had reached the city of Antinoopolis, and had been tortured much by the governor, and yet refused to sacrifice to idols, but rather kept the faith in Christ until death, they were all beheaded—first Irais, and then the rest of the multitude.
Eodem die.
Commemoration of the holy women Xanthippe and Polyxena
Saint Xanthippe was a woman in the time of Claudius Caesar of the Romans; she was the wife of Probus, the governor of Spain, and had a virgin sister, Polyxena by name. When the holy apostle Paul had gone to the region of Spain, and was preaching Christ, Xanthippe came to him; and having been catechized by him in the faith, she was first of all baptized, and then she persuaded her husband, Probus, also to become a Christian. Similarly, her sister Polyxena was also taught by the same apostle at that time; but after his departure, when she heard the great apostle Andrew preaching the faith of the truth in Patras of Achaia, she went to him, and having learned more perfectly the things pertaining to Christ, she received baptism. Having returned again to her own country, she found her sister Xanthippe shining with every kind of virtue, and was received by her with joy. And after both had taught many the faith of Christ, they completed their lives.
IN THE SAME MONTH.
THE TWENTY-FOURTH.
The Contest of the holy protomartyr Thecla.
The protomartyr Thecla was the daughter of a certain noble and wealthy woman from the city of Iconium, by the name of Theoclia. When the holy apostle Paul had gone to Iconium, she heard him teaching the words of God in the house of Onesiphorus, and she was, when she believed, eighteen years of age. She was betrothed in marriage to a wealthy man, Thamyris by name; but despising her mother, her husband, and wealth, she followed the apostle, and having gone with him to Antioch of Pisidia, she was given over to the beasts by the governor Alexander. And having been harmed in nothing, she was delivered to bulls; but having been rescued from these also by the grace of God, she converted many to the Lord, having as a fellow-worker a certain Tryphaena, a God-fearing woman who had received her into her lodging. Having returned again to her own fatherland, she dwelt upon a certain mountain near Seleucia, living a tranquil life and illustrious for the frequency of her miracles. And when certain wicked men desired to go there and violate her, a rock was split into parts and received the saint, who was ninety years of age.
OF THE SAME MONTH. THE TWENTY-FIFTH DAY.
67 Commemoration of the great and terrible earthquake, when a boy was snatched up into the air.
In the reign of Theodosius the Great, an earthquake occurred such as had never happened within human memory. Wherefore the entire populace, together with the emperor and the patriarch, having abandoned the city, went out into the field near the Hebdomon and remained there, because the earthquake was incessant. But while they were carrying supplications from the Hebdomon into the field toward the tribunal, and were adding to the "Holy God, holy mighty, holy immortal" the words "who wast crucified for us"—which were of the heresy of the Theopaschites, who blasphemed by claiming that the Divinity also suffered on the cross—suddenly a boy was snatched up from the midst of the crowd and carried into the air. And while all were crying out in fear, "Lord, have mercy," the boy descended quietly as if by a cloud, and commanded the people to be silent. And he said that the choirs of the angels sing the Trisagion hymn without that addition, "who wast crucified for us." When the boy had said this, he immediately fell dead, and with the heresy, the earthquake also came to an end. And all wondered, giving glory to God.
68 The contest of the holy martyr Paphnutius.
The cruelty of the impious emperor Diocletian made many Christians martyrs, not only from among the laity but also from among the monks; of whose number was the holy Paphnutius. For he, hailing from Egypt, renounced the world and spent the greater part of his life in the deserts. But since he was turning many of the Greeks toward Christ, he was sought after by the governor to be put to death. Many being arrested on his account, he was commanded by an angel, and of his own accord went and stood before the governor; and having confessed Christ, he was bound in iron chains. And being hung upon a beam, he was scraped until his entrails fell to the ground. But he became whole again, an angel having restored these to his body; from this, many believed in Christ. And the governor, seeing this, sent him along with those who had believed to the emperor Diocletian. And by him he was crucified upon a palm tree, and was perfected. And on that same day, four hundred and forty-seven martyrs were beheaded. D
69 Commemoration of the holy Euphrosyne, who was called Smaragdus.
Many are the ways of salvation. For indeed, the holy Euphrosyne, being the daughter of a certain rich man, and intending to be given in marriage, having learned of this, and having set her heart on being the bride of Christ, fled to one of the holy elders, and by him was made a nun. And fearing lest she be recognized by her father, having put on man's clothing and changed her name, she called herself Smaragdus, and departed to a man's mon...
forced into [the form of] a man, and renaming herself Smaragdus, she went off to a male monastery, to which her father also used to go by custom. And after this, when he came again and was recounting to the abbot the flight of his daughter, he saw her many times standing there with the abbot, in the guise of a man, and ostensibly sorrowing on account of his daughter; but he did not recognize her. Having spent eighteen years in the monastery, she fell ill; and having summoned her father, she said, "I am your daughter; do not sorrow." And having said this, she fell asleep.
OF THE SAME MONTH.
XXVI.
The Translation of the holy John the Theologian and Evangelist.
The great Apostle and Evangelist John, after the ascension of the Lord and the repose of the Theotokos, came to Ephesus, preaching the Gospel of Christ. And having been accused before Domitian, the Roman emperor, he was exiled to the island of Patmos. There he also wrote his holy Gospel. After the death of Domitian, being recalled, he returned again to Ephesus. And after teaching many, and leading them to Christ through baptism, he foresaw his own departure. And when the Lord's day arrived, having taught the brethren the mighty works of God, and having commanded them to preserve all things that had been taught to them by him, he ordered his disciple to take men carrying the tools necessary for digging, and to follow him. And having come to the place, he gave orders to dig a deep, cross-shaped pit. And having prayed and said, "Peace be with you, brethren," he lay down in the pit. At that time, the disciples covered him and departed. And after this, when they returned to see him, they did not find him.
OF THE SAME MONTH.
XXVII.
The Contest of the holy martyr Epicharis.
Epicharis, the martyr of Christ, lived in Rome under Diocletian the emperor; she was seized by Caesarius the prefect. And having confessed Christ with boldness, she is hung up and flayed. Thereafter she is struck with leaden hammers by four soldiers; these, while she was praying, were destroyed by angels. Thereafter her fellow-assessor advised the governor to behead her. Having received the sentence by the sword, and while she was praying, the stone that was found beneath her feet gushed forth a multitude of water. And having fulfilled her prayer, and having bowed her neck, she was struck by the executioner, and her spirit was committed into the hands of the living God; for "the souls of the righteous are in the hand of God" (Wisd. III, 1). Her precious body was honorably buried by a certain Felix, a senator and pious Christian; it pours forth healings to all who approach it with faith, readily putting to flight every disease, and every calamity and sorrow, even to this day.
The Same Day: The Contest of the Holy Martyrs Ripsimia, Gaiana, and their Companions.
The martyr Ripsimia, together with Gaiana, were virgins and monastics in the region of Armenia. Gaiana was an old woman, while Ripsimia was young, noble, and of exceptional physical beauty. Learning of her beauty, Tiridates was seized by an uncontrolled passion for her; and having sent for her and captured her, he forced himself upon her for an illicit union. As he did not prevail over her because of the power of Christ, he lay grieving, as if dead. Having pursued the holy woman as she fled, the soldiers caught up with her and compelled her to renounce Christ. When she would not be persuaded, they tore her clothes, bound her hands behind her back, cut out her tongue, gouged out her eyes, and cast her body into the fire; then later, being cut by swords, she surrendered her soul. Along with her, seventy Christian men and thirty-two women were put to death. As for the holy Gaiana, having first had the skin of her entire body flayed and her bones crushed with a stone, she was in this manner beheaded.
MONTH OF OCTOBER: The First Day
The Contest of the Holy Hieromartyr and Apostle Ananias, Bishop of Damascus.
Ananias, the martyr and apostle of Christ, was a disciple of the great apostles Peter and Andrew, and was ordained by them as bishop of the city of Damascus. While he was residing in the same city, he learned that Paul, who was then a persecutor of the Church of Christ, was coming from Jerusalem to arrest him, and he prepared to flee. But the Lord appeared to him and commanded him not to flee, but to remain and baptize him, so that he might recover his sight. For he had been blinded on the road when a light shone around him, and a voice proceeded from the light, saying: "Saul, why do you persecute me? I am Jesus the Christ; but go to Ananias, and be baptized, and you will recover your sight." Having therefore fulfilled the command of the Lord, Ananias baptized Saul, changing his name and calling him Paul. Afterwards, having been captured by the idolaters, he was brought before Lucianus the governor and was beaten, and his sides were flayed; finally, being cast out of the city, he was stoned. And having been perfected, he was buried in that same city of Damascus.
The Same Day: The Contest of the Holy Martyr Michael and his companions, the thirty-six monks.
Saint Michael, with his companions the thirty-six monks, lived a solitary life in the time of the emperors Constantine and Irene, in a monastery near the city of Sebaste. When Alim, the emir of the Saracens, made an incursion into that region, he captured the saints and compelled them to renounce Christ and to accept the superstition of the Aga-
Agarenes. But the holy Fathers were not persuaded; instead, they withstood him, spitting upon him and saying: "We do not fear your tortures, nor do we do your will, for you are enemies of the cross of Christ. Rather, we trample upon your impiety, being strengthened by the power of our Lord Jesus Christ." Then Michael, the most holy hegumen, stood in their midst and exhorted the monks greatly to be constant in their confession of Christ. Therefore, all of them, being confirmed and animated by his teaching, having eagerly bent their necks, were beheaded. And last of all, the most holy Michael, struck by the sword, finished his life.
Eodem die
Commemoration of the holy Romanos, the writer of kontakia.
The holy Romanos was from Syria, being a deacon of the holy Church in Berytus. Having reached Constantinople in the times of the Emperor Anastasius, he went away and stayed in the temple of the most holy Theotokos in the Kyros district, where he also received the gift of the kontakia. For living in blamelessness, and spending the nights in vigil, and praying during the all-night service of the Blachernae, he used to return again to Kyros. Now on one of the nights, while he was sleeping, the most holy Theotokos appeared to him in a dream and handed him a scroll of paper, and said: "Take this paper and eat it." The saint then thought that he opened his mouth and swallowed the paper. It was, in fact, the feast of the holy Nativity of Christ. And immediately having awakened from sleep, he was filled with wonder, and glorified God. Then having ascended to the ambo, he began to sing: "The Virgin today brings forth the Supersubstantial One." And having also composed kontakia for other feasts, about a thousand in number, he departed to the Lord.
Eodem die
Contest of the holy martyr Domninus.
The holy martyr Domninus was from the city of Thessalonica, a pious Christian and one who feared God. When Maximian was building a royal palace in Thessalonica, having been identified as a Christian, he was seized and brought before him. And Maximian said to him with anger: "How is it that, with me present here, you dare to proclaim yourself a Christian, and turning away from the gods, confess another God? But sacrifice to them, if you wish to live." But the saint having said that it is right for every Christian to acknowledge one God and to worship and reverence Him, and to scorn those who are thought to be gods, Maximian ordered the soldiers to lacerate his mouth with whips. And when he saw him remaining unmoved in his conviction toward our Lord Jesus Christ, he ordered him to be taken outside the city and to have his legs broken. And thus shattered, he survived for seven days, having taken no food, and until the very end, giving thanks, he rendered up his spirit.
OF THE SAME MONTH. The Second Day. 84
A 84 The Contest of the holy Cyprian and Justina.
Cyprian, a wise high priest under the Emperor Decius, was born in Antioch of Syria. He was at first a philosopher and a perfect magician. But afterwards he became a Christian for the following reason: A certain virgin lived in that city, Justina by name, who had been baptized by Optatus the bishop. A certain Greek, called Aglaidas, ardently loved her. But when he could not obtain his desire, he fled to Cyprian, asking him to induce Justina to consent to him by means of magic arts. When Cyprian had sent the demons of fornication to solicit the virgin for the third time, he was nevertheless unable to conquer her soul, which was always intent on pouring out prayers to God. But he himself, rather, being seized by the last demon he had sent, understood that the magic art was vain and futile. Wherefore, having burned his books, he was baptized; and he made such progress in virtue that he was ordained bishop, and taught many the faith of Christ. At length, having been arrested together with Justina by the prefect of Damascus, he was beheaded for Christ.
On the same day. 85 The Contest of our holy Father Theophilus the Confessor.
Our holy Father and confessor of Christ, Theophilus, was a pious monk who feared God, in the time of the reign of Leo the Isaurian; whom, when he saw him striving with the greatest zeal to overthrow the holy images and to draw all toward the heresy of the Iconoclasts, he, kindled with divine zeal, approached him: and with a confident spirit rebuked him, calling him a transgressor, a precursor of Antichrist, and a deserter of the orthodox faith. Wherefore, exciting him to wrath and indignation, he was first most severely beaten; then he was cast into prison, so that his body might be consumed by fasting. C Afterwards, being led out of custody, when he was again urged to abjure the cult and adoration of the holy images, and could not be induced to do so, he was sent into exile, in which, having suffered many hardships and very many troubles, and glorifying God for all things, and giving thanks to His holy name, he departed joyfully to Christ.
OF THE SAME MONTH. The Third Day. 85 The Contest of the most holy martyr Dionysius the Areopagite, bishop of Athens.
The great Dionysius in the city of Athens was one of those who excelled in glory, wealth, and wisdom; he was a judge of causes in the place they call the Areopagus. But when the holy apostle Paul had set out for Athens and was preaching Christ, being keen of intellect, he easily understood the truth and believed in Christ. And having been baptized, he was ordained bishop of Athens; teaching many of the Greeks, and returning them to God, and baptizing them. He learned also the secret mysteries of God…
from the holy Hierotheus the bishop, and wrote many books about the heavenly powers. But having been denounced to the prefect of the Athenians by the idolaters, he was seized with his two disciples; and having undergone many torments, he himself was the first to be beheaded. And receiving his head with his own hands, he walked two miles. And he did not put it down until he met a Christian woman, and delivered it to her. Afterwards his two disciples were also beheaded.
IN THE SAME MONTH, THE FOURTH DAY
The contest of the holy martyrs Adauctus and his daughter Callisthenes.
The martyr Adauctus was from the city of Ephesus. He held the rank of duke in the time of the emperor Maximinus, and he had a virgin daughter, very beautiful, named Callisthenes. Maximinus, having heard about her, sent for her to come to him. But her father hid her, sending her away to the regions of the East. When Maximinus learned of this, he was filled with rage, and seized Adauctus under the pretext of Christianity; and having confiscated his property, he exiled him to Mesopotamia, having written to the prefect of Mesopotamia to compel him to sacrifice to the idols; and if he did not obey, to kill him. The prefect, having failed to persuade him, beheaded him. Then he was buried by his wife and daughter. But when Licinius had obtained the empire, Callisthenes approached the empress, and narrated everything. And at the empress’s command, she transferred the remains of her father to Ephesus; where, after she herself had lived a virtuous life, she died.
On the same day
The contest of the holy hieromartyr Peter, bishop of the Capitolei.
This Peter was a Greek; but when he tasted the teaching of Christ, he believed and was baptized together with his wife, his children, and his whole house. Then, reading and learning the divine Scripture, he was ordained bishop of the Church of the Capitolei. And as he was teaching many of the Greeks and returning them to Christ, when a persecution was stirred up against the Christians, he was seized by the idolaters; and having been severely beaten and bound in chains, he was delivered to the governor. The governor, not having persuaded him to sacrifice to the idols, gouged out his eyes, cut out his tongue, severed his ears, and cut the sinews of his hands and feet. After inflicting these torments, he prepared a cross and hung the holy man upon it, while he was praying to God and saying, "Lord, do not lay this sin to their charge." And while he was still breathing, the soldiers came and beheaded him; and thus he completed his life.
A 
Eodem die
89 Dormitio sancti Patris nostri Pauli Simplicis.
Paulus, who was called "the Simple" because of his manner, was a farmer. Having taken a wife who was beautiful in body but corrupt in character, he was returning home one day from the field when he discovered his wife committing adultery. Leaving his home and his children, he went out into the wilderness. He went to the great Antonius and asked to be initiated as a monk. Antonius, observing that he was an old man, said, "An old man of sixty years is not at all able to endure the labors and hardships." Paul replied, "Whatever you bid me, I will do." Antonius therefore made a trial of him, and finding him prepared to endure all things, he tonsured him and made him a monk. He built a cell for him separately in another cave and gave it to him to dwell in. There he applied himself to virtue with such zeal that he was deemed worthy to perform miracles. For when a certain youth possessed by a demon came to Antonius, with Antonius absent, Paul cast the demon out. After these things he ended his life.
Eodem die
90 Certamen sacrosancti martyris Dionysii patriarchæ Alexandrini.
Our holy Father Dionysius flourished in the time of the emperors Valerian and Gallienus. When a persecution against all Christians was stirred up, and especially against the bishops (for the emperors thought that if the bishops were suppressed, the remaining Christians would fall under their power without difficulty), the prefect of Alexandria seized the holy Dionysius. He compelled him to deny Christ and sacrifice to idols; but when he could not be induced to do so by any means, he was relegated to the interior of Egypt so that all opportunity of seeing him would be denied to his friends. In this exile, he did not cease to instruct in the faith of Christ those who came to him. He was not only pelted with stones by the idolaters and tortured with various kinds of torments, but by the order of the prefect he was again deported to a more desolate and inhospitable place. There, having endured many trials, he finally had his holy head severed by the sword.
On the same day
91 Contest of the holy martyrs and deacons Gaius, Faustus, Eusebius, and Chæremon.
These deacons and martyrs were disciples of the holy martyr Dionysius. Gaius and Faustus were his companions in that exile and, after many tortures and tribulations, reached the end of their lives through martyrdom. But Eusebius and Chæremon, after their master and high priest was condemned to exile, strengthened by divine power, visited those saints who were in bonds and ministered to them. They also gathered the relics of the holy martyrs and gave them burial. Thus they continued until the times of the emperor Decius, never withdrawing, but confessing Christ with the greatest freedom, and preaching His saving incarnation to all.
For this reason, having been seized themselves by the idolaters and bound in iron chains, they were delivered to the prefect of Alexandria. And after being subjected to many tortures, finally Eusebius was hanged upon a wooden beam and, having been flayed, was brought to his end. Chæremon for his part expired while being beaten with rods.
On the same day. 92
Commemoration of our holy Father among the saints and hierarch Hierotheus.
Hierotheus, the great hierarch, was one of the nine men who were senators in the city of Athens, and the teacher of Dionysius the Areopagite. Having been instructed in the things concerning Christ by the holy apostles, he followed them, and joined them in singing the doxology, composing and chanting funeral hymns at the falling asleep of our holy and most glorious Lady, the Theotokos and ever-virgin Mary, and he excelled in the sight of the apostles in all things. And he arrived at such a degree of wisdom and knowledge of God that he also, after the holy Dionysius had believed in Christ through the great apostle, Paul, taught him—by the power and co-operation of the all-holy Spirit—concerning the celestial powers, and the angelic orders, and the whole celestial world. He also wrote many other books, discoursing upon great mysteries. Having lived the whole time of his life in piety and virtue, and having served God in many ways, and having been deemed worthy of many gifts, at last he was brought to his end and found rest.
On the same day. C 93
Contest of the holy Dometius the Persian.
The holy Dometius the Persian, in the time of Constantine the Great, was instructed in piety by a certain Christian. Having been baptized, and having left behind parents and wealth, he departed to the monastery of the holy Sergius and Bacchus, near Theodosiopolis; and he became a monk. Where, also, because of his virtue, he was ordained a deacon. And when the archimandrite was at one time celebrating the liturgy, Dometius, holding the rhipidion near the holy table, saw the Holy Spirit in the form of a dove fluttering around the holy gifts and resting upon them; for he had such a pure soul that he could behold the Holy Spirit in bodily form, and his mind was illuminated by Him. When the archimandrite wanted to ordain him a presbyter also, he took to flight and hid himself in a cave, where he benefited many of those who came to him by teaching them. When Julian the Apostate learned of this, he sent men who stoned him to death.
On the same day. 94
Commemoration of the holy Amun the Egyptian.
Amun the God-bearer became a wonder among the ascetics. He was originally from Egypt and was a lover of the monastic life from his youth. But during the times of the emperor Maximian, he withdrew into the desert, and having gone away, he lived in quietude, content with only one garment.
night and day, being nourished by the herbs which the earth bore of its own accord. His life continued until the struggles of the Great Antony; from whom the blessed soul of Amoun, upon departing the body and being carried up to heaven, was seen by the holy Antony while he was sitting and teaching the brethren some soul-profiting words. Suddenly rising, he looked upon it being escorted by angels and glorified God. Those who were present asked him, and having learned the matter, they noted the hour; and upon departing for Egypt, after ten days they found that the holy Amoun had fallen asleep at that very hour which Antony had spoken of.
ON THE SAME DAY.
The Contest of Saint Mamelchtha the Martyr.
Saint Mamelchtha was of Persian origin and a priestess of the temple of Diana, but she had a Christian sister. Having lapsed into sleep, she saw an angel of God showing her the mysteries of the Christians. And having awakened in fear, she related it to her sister. She brought her to the bishop. And the bishop, having understood the symbol of the vision, said to her: "It behoves you to become a Christian." Whom he immediately baptized, and her own sister received her. Wherefore the Gentiles, having been moved to madness, punished her and killed her with stones, while she was still clad in the shining garments of holy baptism. And they cast her into a very deep pit, from which the Christians with difficulty retrieved her. The bishop went to the king of the Persians and requested, and obtained, authority to tear down the temple of Diana and to build a church in the name of the holy martyr; which, having built, he deposited her precious relics within it.
ON THE SAME DAY.
The Contest of Saint Charitina the Martyr.
The martyr of Christ, Charitina, lived during the time of the emperor Diocletian and the count Domitius, being the slave of a certain Claudius. Concerning her, the rumor having reached the count, he wrote to her master that she be sent unto him, because an inquiry was to be held concerning her as a Christian. Her master was grieved, and having donned sackcloth, he lamented her fate. But she comforted her master, saying: "Do not grieve, my lord. For I shall offer my remains for mine own and your sins." And he said: "Be mindful of me before the heavenly King." Therefore, she was sent to the count; and having professed herself to be a Christian, her head was shaven and sprinkled with burning coals. Then, with a heavy stone hung around her neck, she was thrown into the sea. And she received the water for baptism; and having emerged, she appeared to the count. And those standing by, seeing her, were astonished. Then, having had her teeth pulled out and the fingers of her hands and feet cut off, she was perfected.
ON THE SAME DAY.
The Contest of Saint Thomas the Apostle.
After the ascension of our Lord Jesus Christ, each of the holy apostles having gone forth into the country allotted to him, taught...
The region of the Indians fell to holy Thomas, where he preached Christ. Now, when the wife of the king of the Indians, along with her son, had believed in Christ through his word, Thomas was detected, and by the king's order, he was shut up in prison along with other prisoners. But after the king's son and his mother, and others not a few, had given money to the soldiers, they entered in to him in the prison and were baptized. And after a sufficient time, presbyters and deacons were ordained from among them, and they taught in the name of Christ. And when the king learned of this, he was angered; he led the apostle out of the prison and, having handed him over to soldiers, ordered them to kill him. Taking him, therefore, to the mountain, they pierced the holy man with spears and slew him.
On the same day
Commemoration of our holy Father Nicetas the Confessor.
Our holy Father and confessor of Christ, Nicetas, was a kinsman of the Empress Irene, to whom he was acceptable not so much for their kinship as for his own virtue. For he excelled in the contests and exercises of virtue to such a degree that he was sent by the Empress as her legate to the holy Second Council of Nicaea, in which office he conducted himself excellently and remarkably, as was pleasing to God. For he was most capable of performing all things splendidly. Wherefore, being heaped with the honor of a patrician, he was appointed strategist of Sicily; and having administered that province very well, and having bestowed mercy upon widows and orphans, he returned home. But finding that the Iconoclast Leo the Armenian had seized the empire, he abandoned his rank, his wealth, and all the glory of the world and became a monk. And when the emperor learned of this, wishing to punish him, he sent word to him to send an icon of the Saviour, that he might burn it. But since he refused to do this, he sent him into exile; and having been subjected to many sufferings in exile, he reached his end.
ON THE SAME MONTH.
THE SEVENTH.
The Contest of the holy martyrs Sergius and Bacchus.
Sergius and Bacchus, the glorious martyrs of Christ, flourished under the Emperor Maximian; they were handsome of form and held in high regard by the emperor himself. Sergius was a primicerius, and Bacchus was second to him, but both were pious and worshippers of Christ. For this reason, having been denounced to the emperor, they were compelled to sacrifice to the idols. And as they would not agree to this, they were first stripped of their offices and their torments, and having been divested of their manly attire, they were clothed in women's garments and led publicly through the midst of the city. Then they were sent to the governor of Augustopolis, to be punished by him. But along the road, an angel of God walked before them, guiding them. Then, after they were brought to the governor, Bacchus, having entered the contest first, died under the very torments; then Sergius c—
having had his feet pierced with iron shoes with sharp nails, and being forced to run, finished his life by the sword.
The contest of the holy martyr Pelagia.
Pelagia the martyr lived in the time of the emperor Diocletian, from the city of Tarsus. While Clinus the bishop was baptizing many of the Greeks, the holy Pelagia saw in a dream the form of the bishop exhorting her toward baptism. Therefore, having asked permission from her mother to go as if to her nurse, she went to the bishop and was baptized. After her baptism, she gave her precious robe to the bishop to be distributed to the poor. After this, when she returned to her mother, she was not received by her. But she, seeing her in a humble garment, burst into tears, and, hastening, she reported to the son of Diocletian, who had betrothed Pelagia as his wife, saying that Pelagia had become a Christian. When the young man heard this, and being overcome with grief, he laid hands upon himself. Wherefore Diocletian, his father, inflamed with anger against the saint, ordered her to be seized and cast into a bronze ox made red-hot with fire and burned.
ON THE SAME MONTH. The eighth day. Commemoration of another holy Pelagia, the virgin of Antioch.
Holy Pelagia, in the time of the emperor Numerian, was born of high status in the city of Antioch in Syria. When the prefect of the city learned that she was a Christian, he sent soldiers to apprehend her. When the soldiers had gone and surrounded her house, intending to seize her, the saint, observing this, asked that they wait a little while until she finished dressing and came down. When they agreed to her promise, she, standing toward the east in the place where she was accustomed to pray, with hands and eyes lifted to heaven, prayed for a long time to God that she might not fall into the power of the soldiers, but that before she was apprehended, she might depart to Him chaste and pure. Having said these things, and having modestly composed herself, she surrendered her spirit to the Lord. The prefect, together with those with him, when he learned of this, was astonished and marveled.
On the same day. Commemoration of the holy Pelagia, the former harlot.
The most holy patriarch of great Antioch once gathered the bishops under him for a certain ecclesiastical matter. Among their number was also Nonnus, the holy bishop. While this Nonnus was teaching the people, a certain harlot named Pelagia passed by with her retinue, adorned with precious stones, and pearls, and gold, and the rest of her finery; so that even the air was filled
with perfume, and as the other bishops turned their eyes away, Nonnus alone fixed his eyes intently upon her. Then, weeping, he said that God, in His love for mankind, would surely receive her. And after this, when the bishop began to teach again, Pelagia listened along with the people; and she was so pricked in heart that she was baptized, and gave all her wealth to the poor. Then, having gone to Jerusalem and become a nun, she attained such boldness before God that she performed many miracles, and thus was perfected.
ON THE SAME MONTH. The Ninth.
The contest of the holy martyrs Juventinus and Maximus.
The martyrs of Christ, Juventinus and Maximus, lived during the times of Julian the Apostate. They were infantrymen in arms, who were accustomed to march before him as he traveled, and had won his favor because of their bravery. But when they were in the city of Antioch in Syria, and he had commanded that the springs, and the rivers, and the food for sale be defiled and stained with the blood of sacrifices—so that those who tasted them might, even against their will, be made participants in the sacrifice to idols—the holy ones, seeing this, groaned and wept. And when they were reclining at the table and eating, they wept even more fervently, and said to God: "Why, O Lord, hast Thou delivered us unto an apostate and lawless emperor, more than all the nations?" Someone among those at the table heard this and reported it to Julian. C He, filled with wrath, immediately sent and had the holy ones brought before him. And having first subjected them to many grievous tortures, and failing to persuade them to deny Christ, he later ordered their heads to be cut off.
On the same day. Commemoration of the holy deaconess Publia.
Holy Publia lived during the times of Julian the Apostate; she was of noble birth and a deaconess of the Church of Antioch in Syria, renowned for her virtues. For a short time she had lived in matrimony and had given birth to the most holy presbyter John; having a choir of virgins, she sang hymns to God. One day, as she was chanting the psalm in a loud voice, which says: "The idols of the nations are silver and gold, the work of men's hands; let those who make them become like them, and all who put their trust in them!" Julian, who was passing by and heard this, commanded her to be silent. But Publia was so far from being silenced that she sang even more loudly. For this reason the Apostate, in his anger, had her brought to him and commanded the soldiers to strike her on the face. She, having reproved his impiety, returned to her house and, as was her custom, sang psalms and glorified God with the rest of the virgins. Finally, having lived a most excellent life, she departed in peace.
The same day. The Contest of the Holy Dorotheus, Archbishop of the City of Tyre.
Dorotheus, a most celebrated man, became bishop of the city of Tyre; he was well-versed in all scriptural history. He had indeed fled because of the persecution. But after the death of Diocletian and Licinius, he returned to Tyre again and governed the holy Church of God until the time of Julian the Apostate. And since Julian, not openly but secretly, caused Christians to be put to death by his prefects, the holy Dorotheus, having heard this, withdrew from Tyre and reached the region of Thrace. But not even there could he escape the death at the hands of the idolaters. For, having been seized by the prefects of Julian and having endured many tortures—even though he was an old man, already one hundred and seven years of age—in the midst of those very torments, he died for his confession of Christ, and he surrendered his blessed soul to Christ, having previously composed and left behind many writings of no small utility.
The same day. The Contest of the holy apostle James of Alphaeus.
James, the apostle of Christ, was the son of Alphaeus and brother of Matthew the publican and evangelist. He preached the word of Christ and taught all who came to him, both Jews and Greeks, things pertaining to His eternal kingdom, and baptized a great multitude of people in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit. The Jews, bearing this with indignation, formed a plan to kill him. Wherefore, having gathered together in one place, they seized him, and after having cruelly and dreadfully tortured him, they finally slew him by beating him with rods; while in the meantime the saint, until his final breath, offered praises to our Lord Jesus Christ and gave thanks to His holy Name, which had helped and cooperated with him to confound and conquer the devil and those who do his will. And having thus surrendered his blessed soul, he was buried.
OF THE SAME MONTH. The Tenth Day. The Contest of the holy martyrs Eulampius and Eulampia.
Eulampius, the martyr of Christ, and his sister Eulampia were from the city of Nicomedia, under the Emperor Maximian. To avoid the persecution, they had hidden themselves with many other Christians in the mountains. But Eulampius, on going down to the city to buy provisions, saw the imperial edicts posted in the forum. While he stood reading these, he was seized by the idol-worshipers and brought before the emperor. Having been forced to enter the temple of idols to offer sacrifice, he entered and prayed to Christ; and the idols fell down and were shattered. As he was being tortured for this, his sister Eulampia, learning what had happened, descended from the
Because of this the Persians seized him, and tortured him severely. And because of the many bonds, he was like one dead, barely breathing. And lying there, he opened his eyes, and said to those who stood by: "Arise, and make prayers to God; for behold, I see a ladder reaching to heaven, and fiery men upon it, saying to me: 'Come with us, and we shall lead you into a city full of joy, and gladness, and light.'" With these words he breathed his last: and his holy soul flew up to the heavenly city, which he had seen. But his body lies buried in Persia, awaiting the final and terrible resurrection, when he too shall rise with all others, and enjoy the immortal kingdom of Christ our God.
On the same month. The second day.
Commemoration of the holy and blessed Philaret the Merciful.
In the reign of the most religious Empress Irene, this man flourished; originating from the region of Paphlagonia, he possessed indeed quite ample wealth, which he had gathered for himself through his own labor; but afterwards, through diabolical envy, he was reduced to poverty, yet he did not therefore forget almsgiving. From one example of his beneficence, it will be made plain how incredible was his liberality towards the poor. Once, the ox of his neighbor had wandered off: the neighbor approached Philaretus, and found him in his field sowing with oxen joined under the yoke. But Philaretus, seeing the man overcome with sorrow, unyoked one of the oxen and gave it to him, and he himself took the yoke upon himself with the other ox and returned home. God, intending to reward this virtue, brought it about that the empress arranged for Maria, his granddaughter, to be wedded to her son Constantine. By which it came to pass that his family was enriched. But he himself became more inclined towards the poor, and flourishing until old age, he died, and was buried in the monastery of Kriseos.
On the same day.
Commemoration of the holy prophet Habakkuk.
This man was of the tribe of Simeon from the region of Judea: he prophesied concerning the captivity and the destruction of Jerusalem, the city of the Hebrews, and about the slaughter and the servitude of the people. But when Nebuchadnezzar, the king of the Persians, had come against Jerusalem, he fled and went to a foreign land; and as soon as the Persians retreated, and those of the Jews who remained had gone down into Egypt, he returned himself also to his own land, and was serving the reapers of his field: and when he was once bringing lunch to them, he said to his own household: "I am going a long path: but if I delay for a longer time, take food yourselves to the reapers." Having said these things, he was caught up by an angel to Babylon, which was distant twelve hundred and sixty-five miles, and he gave the food to the prophet Daniel, who was shut up in the pit: and being carried home again, he brought another lunch to the reapers, having disclosed the matter to no one.
